where \(\mathcal{P}\) is predictable, \(\sigma\)-algebra, that is the sub-\(\sigma\)-algebra of \(\mathcal{F}\otimes\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R}_+)\) generated by adapted, left-continuous processes. \cref{rem:L^2(M)} now reads as the statement that 
the measures
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{P}_{[M]}(B):=\mathbb E\left[\int_0^{\infty} 1_B\,\diff [M]\right],
\qquad
\mathbb{P}_{\langle M\rangle}(B):=\mathbb E\left[\int_0^{\infty} 1_B\,\diff \langle M\rangle\right]
\end{equation*}
coincide on the predictable \(\sigma\)-algebra. In turn, this justifies the notation \(\mathbb{P}_M\) indifferent to whether \(\langle M\rangle\) or \([M]\) is used.


Equation \eqref{eq:L^2(M) as Hilbert space}  implies \(L^2(M)\) is a Hilbert space with inner product \((X,Y)_{L^2(M)}=\mathbb{E}[\int_0^{\infty}X Y\diff\langle M\rangle_t]\).
 
We turn to the proof that the processes in \(L^2(M)\) are approximated by elementary predictable processes. The proof will be based on an application of the functional version of the monotone class (see theorem \parencite[Thm.~7.4.1.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}), which crucially uses the following fact
\begin{equation*}
    \sigma(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{P}. 
\end{equation*}
First, we show a simple but useful lemma.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:sigma algebra of bounded functions}
    Let \(\mathcal{M}\) be a class of real-valued functions on \(\Omega\). We denote by \(b\mathcal{M}\) the class of essentially bounded functions in \(\mathcal{M}\). Then
    \begin{equation*}
        \sigma(b\mathcal{M})=\sigma(\mathcal{M}).
    \end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    The inclusion \(\sigma(b\mathcal{M})\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{M})\) is clear. For the converse, let us consider \(X\in\mathcal{M}\) and define
    \begin{equation*}
        X^n = -n\vee X\wedge n\in b \mathcal{M}.
    \end{equation*}
    It is clear that \(X^n\to X\) pointwise as \(n\to \infty\), that is \(X^n(\omega,t)\to X(\omega,t)\) for all \((\omega,t)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\). Because pointwise limits preserve measurability, \(X\) is \(\sigma(b\mathcal{M})\)-measurable as the pointwise limit of processes in \(b\mathcal{M}\). Because \(\sigma(\mathcal{M})\) is the smallest \(\sigma\)-algebra such that all \(X\in\mathcal{M}\) are measurable, the claim follows.
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
    We somtimes abuse language in saying that a process is bounded when we mean that it is essentially bounded. In our cases, we can always restrict ourselves to a full measure set on which the essentially bounded function/process is bounded. 
\end{remark}
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:elementary processes generated predictable sigma algebra}
    For \(\mathcal{E}\) as in Definition \ref{def:elementary predictable process} and \(\mathcal{P}\) denoting the predictable \(\sigma\)-algebra, we have that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \sigma(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{P}.
    \end{equation*}
    \begin{proof}
        By definition, \(\mathcal{P}\) is generated by the family of adapted, left-continuous processes. Every element of \(\mathcal{E}\) is clearly adapted and left-continuous; hence, \(\sigma(\mathcal{E})\subseteq\mathcal{P}\) is clear. It remains to show that \(\mathcal{P}\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{E})\). For this, we first show that every bounded, adapted left-continuous process is the limit of elements of \(\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\). By Lemma \ref{lemma:sigma algebra of bounded functions} the boundedness assumption is without loss of generality.
        
        
           Let \(X\) be a bounded, adapted, left-continuous process. We define
        \begin{equation}\label{eq:approximating adapted left-continuous process}
            X^n= X_01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{k\geq 1}X_{k/n}1_{(k/n,(k+1)/n]}
        \end{equation}
        As \(X\) is adapted and bounded, \(X_{k/n}\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{k/n}\)-measurable and bounded; thus, \(X^n\in\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\). Then, for every fixed \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\), there exists a unique \(k(n)\in\mathbb{N}\) such that \(\frac{k(n)}{n}<t\leq \frac{k(n)+1}{n}\). Hence, \(X_t^n=X_{k(n)/n}\), thus \(X_t^n\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{k(n)/n}\subseteq\mathcal{F}_t\)-measurable; hence, it is adapted. Furthermore, notice that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{k(n)}{n}\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}|\frac{k(n)}{n}-t|+t\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n}+t=t\) and \(\frac{k(n)}{n}<t\); hence, \(k(n)\nearrow t\). Thus, for every \((\omega,t)\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+,\;X^n_t(\omega)\to X_t(\omega)\) as \(n\to\infty\) by the left-continuity of \(X\). This implies 
        \begin{equation*}
            \mathcal{P}\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{E}^{\infty}).
        \end{equation*}
        Indeed, every bounded, adapted, left-continuous process \(X\) is \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}^{\infty})\) as the point-wise limit of elements in \(\mathcal{E}^{\infty}\). But \(\mathcal{P}\) is the smallest such \(\sigma\)-algebra.



 Furthermore, every 
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:approximating adapted left-continuous processes 2}
     \vartheta=\vartheta^01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{i= 0}^\infty \vartheta^i1_{(t_i,t_{i+1}]}\in\mathcal{E}^{\infty}
 \end{equation}
 is clearly the pointwise limit of the processes
        \begin{equation*}\label{eq:approximating approximating-sequence of adapted left-continuous processes}
            \vartheta^{m}=\vartheta^01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{k=1}^m\vartheta^{i}1_{(t_k,t_{k+1}]}\in\mathcal{E},
        \end{equation*}
        for \(m\to\infty\).
        Again as pointwise limits of measurable processes are measurable we have
        \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{P}\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{E}^{\infty})\subseteq\sigma(\mathcal{E}).
        \end{equation*}
        \end{proof}
\end{lemma}
Taking a closer look, we see that the previous proof can be used to show the following usefull corollary.
\begin{corollary}
    Let \(\mathcal{A}=\left\{A\times(s,t], A\in\mathcal{F}_t, s<t\in\mathbb{R}_+\right\}\cup \left\{\left\{0\right\}\times A, A\in\mathcal{F}_0\right\}\). Then \(\sigma(\mathcal{A})=\mathcal{P}\).
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    Every indicator function \(1_B=1_A1_{(s,t]}\) with \(B\in\mathcal{A}\) is adapted and left-continuous; hence, it is predictable. The converse inclusion follows from the very same argument as in Lemma \ref{lemma:elementary processes generated predictable sigma algebra}. Indeed, every bounded, adapted left-continuous process \(X\) is the limit of processes \(X^n\) of the form \eqref{eq:approximating adapted left-continuous process}. Every \(X^n\) is the limit of processes \(X^n_m\) of the form \eqref{eq:approximating adapted left-continuous processes 2}. The latter is clearly seen to be \(\sigma(\mathcal{A})\)-measurable as \(X_{k/n}\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{k/n}\)-measurable. Indeed, for \(B\in\mathcal{B}(\mathbb{R})\)
    \begin{align*}
    (X_{\frac{k}{n}}1_{(\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}]})^{-1}(B)=
    \begin{cases}
        X_{\frac{k}{n}}^{-1}(B)\times (\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}] \text{ if }0\notin B\\
        X_{\frac{k}{n}}^{-1}(B)\times (\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}]\cup \Omega\times \mathbb{R}/(\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}]\text{ if }0\in B
    \end{cases}
    \end{align*}
    but \( \Omega\times \mathbb{R}/(\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}]=\big(\Omega\times(\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}]\big)^c\in\sigma(\mathcal{A})\). In every case
    \((X_{\frac{k}{n}}1_{(\frac{k}{n},\frac{k}{n}+\frac{1}{n}]})^{-1}(B)\in\sigma(\mathcal{A})\).
    Hence, \(X^n_m\) is \(\sigma(\mathcal{A})\)-measurable as a finite sum of measurable processes, and \(X^n\) is \(\sigma(\mathcal{A})\)-measurable as a pointwise limit of measurable processes; hence, so is \(X\).
\end{proof}
We can finally state and prove the density of elementary processes in \(L^2(M)\).
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) and \(\mathcal{E}\) be defined in Definition \ref{def:elementary predictable process}. Then \(\mathcal{E}\) is dense in \(L^2(M)\).
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    The proof is based on the functional version of the monotone class theorem and dominated convergence. By Lemma \ref{lemma:sigma algebra of bounded functions} and Lemma \ref{lemma:elementary processes generated predictable sigma algebra} we have 
    \begin{equation*}
        \sigma(\mathcal{E})=\mathcal{P}.
    \end{equation*}
    Let 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{H}=\left\{ \vartheta\in b\mathcal{P}:\vartheta\in L^2(M),\forall \epsilon>0, \exists \vartheta_{\epsilon}\in \mathcal{E}\text{ with }\lVert \vartheta_{\epsilon}-\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon\right\},
    \end{equation*}
    where \(b\mathcal{P}\) denotes the set of processes that are essentially bounded and predictable.

    
      Suppose we were able to show that \(\mathcal{H}\) is a monotone class containing the algebra of functions \(\mathcal{E}\). We would conclude by the monotone class theorem  that \(\mathcal{H}\) contains all bounded \(\mathcal{P}\)-measurable processes, which would show that \(\mathcal{E}\) is dense in \(bL^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\). To get density in \(L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\) we observe that for \(\vartheta\in L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\) we might consider, without loss of generality, the bounded process
    \begin{equation*}
        \vartheta^n=-n\vee \vartheta\wedge n\in bL^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M).
    \end{equation*}
 Clearly, \(\vartheta^n\mapsto \vartheta\) pointwise and \(|\vartheta^n|\leq|\vartheta|\) hence \(|\vartheta^n-\vartheta|\leq 2|\vartheta|\) with \(\vartheta\in L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\) by assumption. Thus, by dominated convergence
    \begin{equation*}
        \vartheta^n\overset{L^2(M)}{\to}\vartheta.
    \end{equation*}
    In particular, for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exists \(n\) large enough such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert \vartheta^n-\vartheta\rVert<\frac{\epsilon}{2}.
    \end{equation*}
    Furthermore, if \(\mathcal{E}\) is dense in \(bL^2(M)\) there exists a sequence \((\xi^n_m)_{m\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathcal{E}\) such that 
    \begin{equation*}
         \xi^n_m\overset{L^2(M)}{\to}\vartheta^n.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence for every \(\epsilon>0\) there is \(m\) big enough such that
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert \xi^n_m-\vartheta^n\rVert<\frac{\epsilon}{2}.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, for these choices for \(n,m\)
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert \xi^n_m-\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}\leq\lVert \xi^n_m-\vartheta^n\rVert_{L^2(M)}+\lVert \vartheta^n-\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon.
    \end{equation*}
    In other words, we just argued that \(bL^2(M)\) is dense in \(L^2(M)\); hence
    \begin{equation*}
        \overline{\mathcal{E}}^{L^2(M)}=\overline{bL^2(M)}^{L^2(M)}=L^2(M).
    \end{equation*}
 It remains to show that the application of the monotone class theorem is justified. 

 
      First, we need to show that \(\mathcal{H}\) contains \(\mathcal{E}\). Clearly, processes in \(\mathcal{E}\) are predictable. Furthermore, as \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\), we have by \eqref{eq:P_M is finite measure} that \([M]_\infty\) is integrable. Hence, as elements in \(\mathcal{E}\) are bounded, uniformly in \((\omega, t)\) on \(\mathcal{N}^c\times\mathbb{R}_+\), where \(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{N})=0\) we have for \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}\)
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff [M]_t\bigg]\lesssim\mathbb{E}\big[[M_\infty]\big]<\infty.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, \(\mathcal{H}\) contains \(\mathcal{E}\). Notice, that this crucially uses the integrability of \([M]_\infty\) which, according to \eqref{eq:P_M is finite measure} is the case if and only if \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\). In particular, it is not enough to require \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\).  
    
    What is more, \(\mathcal{H}\) contains the constant processes. Indeed, let \(c\in\mathbb{R}\) and  \(X_t(\omega)=c\) for all \((\omega,t)\in\Omega\times \mathbb{R}_+\) and consider \(X^n=c1_{\left\{0\right\}}+c1_{(0,n]}\). Then \(X^n\in \mathcal{E}\) and \(X^n\to X\) pointwise. Furthermore, as the measure \(\mathbb{P}_M\) is finite for \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\) we have that the constant process equal to \(c^2\) is in \(L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\). It is then a direct application of dominated convergence that yields 
    \begin{equation*}
        X^n\overset{L^2(M)}{\to}X.
    \end{equation*}
In particular, for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exists \(n\) such that 
\begin{equation*}
    \lVert X^n-c\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon.
\end{equation*}
Clearly, \(\mathcal{H}\) forms a vector space of functions (on \(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\)). Indeed, if \(\vartheta_1,\vartheta_2\in\mathcal{H}\), for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exist \(\vartheta^1_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}},\vartheta^2_{\frac{\epsilon}{2|\alpha|}}\in\mathcal{E}\) such that, for all \(\alpha\in\mathbb{R}\):
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert \vartheta^1+\alpha \vartheta^2- \vartheta^1_{\frac{\epsilon}{2}}-\alpha \vartheta^2_{\frac{\epsilon}{2|\alpha|}} \rVert_{L^2(M)}\leq \epsilon,
    \end{equation*}
    due to the triangle inequality.
 It remains to show that \(\mathcal{H}\) is closed under bounded monotone limits. Hence, let \(0<\vartheta_0<\vartheta_1<\dots\) with \(\vartheta_j\in\mathcal{H}\) such that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta_n = \vartheta\) pointwise, with \(\vartheta\) bounded. Fix \(\epsilon>0\). Then, due to dominated convergence 
    \begin{equation*}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\lVert \vartheta-\vartheta_n\rVert_{L^2(M)}=0.
    \end{equation*}
    Hence, there exists \(n\in\mathbb{N}\) large enough such that \(\lVert \vartheta-\vartheta_n\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon/2\). Now \(\vartheta_n\in\mathcal{\vartheta}\); hence, there exists \(\vartheta^n_{\epsilon/2}\in \mathcal{E}\) such that \(\lVert \vartheta_n-\vartheta^n_{\epsilon/2}\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon/2\). By the triangle inequality, we have
    \begin{equation}
        \lVert \vartheta-\vartheta^n_{\epsilon/2}\rVert_{L^2(M)}\leq\lVert \vartheta-\vartheta_n\rVert_{L^2(M)}+\lVert \vartheta_n-\vartheta^n_{\epsilon/2}\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon
    \end{equation}
 Hence, we conclude by the monotone class theorem. 
\end{proof}
One might wonder whether the latter can be generalized to general \(p\in[1,\infty)\). The proof given does not hold for the general case without modification. Indeed, the main reason for giving the proof in the case \(p=2\) is that the proof remains tractable, as the repeated application of dominated convergence can be done on the joint space \((\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+)\), as \(L^2(M)\simeq L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P_M})\) forms a Hilbert space. For \(p\neq 2\), however, there is no such \(L^p\)-identification. We need to show how to adapt the dominated convergence arguments in the previous proof. The rest is analogous and does not depend on the choice of \(p\). Let's take a look at the place we used dominated convergence (DCT) in the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class}. In the following we refer to the case where we allow for and \(p\in[1,\infty)\) as the general case. For the following asseum \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p_0\).


The first time we used DCT was to argue that it is enough to show the claim for bounded functions in \(L^2(M)\). In the general case, we argue by successive application of DCT. 
Again, assume the claim to be shown for bounded processes in \(L^p(M)\). Let \(\vartheta\in L^p(M)\) and consider \(\vartheta^n=-n\vee \vartheta\wedge n\in bL^p(M)\). Again, it is clear that \(\vartheta^n\to\vartheta\) pointwise with \(|\vartheta^n-\vartheta|\leq 2|\vartheta|\). First notice, that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:integral is integrable}
    \mathbb{E}\Big[\left(\int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta|_s^2\diff [M]_s\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\Big]<\infty\implies\int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta|_s^2\diff [M]_s<\infty,\;\;\mathbb{P}\text{-almost everywhere}
\end{equation}
 Furthermore, observe that \([M]\) is a non-negative, non-decreasing process, hence for every \(\omega\), \(\diff [M](\omega)\) defines a Lebsegue-Stieljes measure. As \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p\), the BDG inequalities imply that \(\diff [M]\) is, almost surely, a finite measure. 
 Indeed
 \begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}\big[[M]^{\frac{p}{2}}\big]\leq \frac{1}{c_p}\mathbb{E}\Big[\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}|M|^p\Big]=\lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^p}<\infty,
\end{equation*}
which implies that \([M]<\infty\) almost surely. 
Hence outside of a \(\mathbb{P}\)-nullset  \(\mathcal{N}\) we can apply DCT for every \(\omega\), that is pathwise, with the domination of \(\vartheta^2\in L^1(\diff [M](\omega)) \) to obtain
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_{n\to\infty}\int_{0}^{\infty}|\vartheta_s^n(\omega)-\vartheta_s(\omega)|^2\diff [M]_s(\omega)=0.
\end{equation*}
Next, as \([M]\) is non-decreasing,
\(\int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta_s^n-\vartheta|_s^2\diff [M]_s\) is dominated by \(\int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta|_s^2\diff [M]_s\) which is in \(L^1(\mathbb{P})\) by the observation made in \eqref{eq:integral is integrable}. The same bounds are clearly true for the \(\frac{p}{2}\)-power of the respective terms. Hence, again by DCT we conclude.
\begin{equation*}
    \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[\int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta^n_s-\vartheta_s|^2\diff[M]_s=0
\end{equation*}
Together, we get
\begin{equation*}
    \vartheta^n\overset{L^p(M)}{\to}\vartheta.
\end{equation*}
For the assertion that the constant functions are in
\begin{equation*}
        \mathcal{H}^{(p)}=\left\{ \vartheta\in b\mathcal{P}:\vartheta\in L^p(M),\forall \epsilon>0, \exists \vartheta_{\epsilon}\in \mathcal{E}\text{ with }\lVert \vartheta_{\epsilon}-\vartheta\rVert_{L^p(M)}<\epsilon\right\}.
\end{equation*}
we observe that the constant process \(X\equiv c\in L^p(M)\). Indeed 
\begin{equation*}
    \mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_0^{\infty}c^2\diff [M]\right)^{\frac{p}{2}}\bigg]=|c|^{p}\mathbb{E}\big[[M]_\infty^{\frac{p}{2}}\big]<\infty,
\end{equation*}
where the finiteness of the last term is due to the BDG inequalities. Hence, setting  \(X^n=c1_{\left\{0\right\}}+c1_{(0,n]}\), with the obvious bound
\begin{equation*}
    |X^n|\leq |c|
\end{equation*}
we argue by the same successive DCT argument as before that 
\begin{equation*}
    X^n\overset{L^p(M)}{\to}c.
\end{equation*}
 The stability under bounded monotone limits follows analogously. Hence, we get the following proposition:
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:density of E in Lp(M) via monotone class}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}^p_0\) for \(p\in[1,\infty)\) and \(\mathcal{E}\) as defined in Definition \ref{def:elementary predictable process}. Then \(\mathcal{E}\) is dense in \(L^p(M)\).
\end{proposition}
\bigbreak
Finally, having proven that the elementary processes are dense in \(L^2(M)\), we can use the isometry property for the map \eqref{eq:integral map as isometry} to extend the integral to a bounded linear operator in \(\overline{\mathcal{E}}^{L^2(M)}=L^2(M)\) by the standard extension theorem (see \parencite[Prop.~5.4]{SteinShakarchi2005RealAnalysis}). This uses, in particular, that \(L^2(M)\) and \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) are Banach spaces with respect to their norms. This follows from the fact that they can both be dentified with a \(L^2\) space. Hence, as Hilbert spaces, they are in particular Banach.
We get the following theorem.
\begin{theorem}\label{def:square-integrable stochastic integral}
    The linear isometry \(H\mapsto \int H\diff M\) of \(\mathcal{E}\) to \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) extends uniquely to an linear isometry from \(L^2(M)\) to \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). We call this extension the integral of \(H\) with respect to \(M\) and denote it equivalently as \(I_M(H)\), \(H\bullet M\) or \(\int H\diff M\).
\end{theorem}
Notice that as we define the stochastic integral as the extension of a map \(I:\mathcal{E}\to\mathcal{H}_0^2\) to \(L^2(M)\), where \(\mathcal{E}\subseteq L^2(M)\) is a dense subset, the resulting integral is in \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) by construction. 


The isometry property of the integral in Theorem \ref{def:square-integrable stochastic integral} is generally known as Itô's isometry. According to \parencite[Thm.~2.5.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}, it even holds when \(M\) is only locally in \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). 
\begin{theorem}[Itô Isometry]\label{thm:Ito Isometry}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2\); then for \(H\in L^2(M)\)
    \begin{equation*}
        \lVert H\rVert_{L^2(M)}^2=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}H_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_0^{\infty}H_t\diff M_t\right)^2\bigg].
    \end{equation*}
\end{theorem}
We will refer to the setting in Definition \ref{def:square-integrable stochastic integral} as the \textit{square-integrable setting}. We provide a useful characterization of the integral in Definition \ref{def:square-integrable stochastic integral}
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:characterisation of stochastic integral}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\) and \(H\in L^2(M)\). The stochastic integral \(H\bullet M\) is the unique process in \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) such that, for every \(N\in\mathcal{H}^2\),
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:Schweizer integral characterization}
        \langle M\bullet N,N\rangle=\int H\diff\langle M,N\rangle,\forall N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2.
    \end{equation}
\end{proposition}
Proposition \ref{prop:characterisation of stochastic integral} might be used as an alternative definition of the stochastic integral of \(H\in L^2(M)\) and \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\). In particular one can show Proposition \ref{prop:characterisation of stochastic integral} for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\). This shows that one can construct the stochastic integral \(\int H\diff M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\) and \(H\in L^2(M)\). This is extension is not clear from our construction as we crucially used \(\mathcal{E}\subset L^2(M)\) which used the integrability of \([M]_{\infty}\), which is the case if and only if \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\). 
For the proof of Proposition \ref{prop:characterisation of stochastic integral}, we refer to \parencite[Thm.~2.5.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}. Moreover, \parencite[sec.~4.2.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus} shows that the Itô-isometry also holds for the integral \(\int H\diff M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\) and \(H\in L^2(M)\).


Nevertheless we can give some insight in how to interpret \eqref{prop:characterisation of stochastic integral}. Observe that the right-hand side of \eqref{eq:Schweizer integral characterization} defines a linear form in \(N\), hence on \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). The latter has been identified with a Hilbert space. By the Kunita-Watanabe inequality (see \parencite[Thm.11.4.1]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications})  it is continuous a linear map. By the Riesz representation theorem for Hilbert spaces there exists a unique support element \(L\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\) such that the continuous linear form can be written as an inner product with \(L\). The latter characterizes the continuous linear form on a Hilbert space uniquely. One then defines the stochastic integral \(H\bullet M \coloneqq L\) and justifies this definition by the properties of \(L\).

Returning to our construction of the integral for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) and \(H\in L^2(M)\) one can  extend the integral to integrators in \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\) and integrands in \(H\in L^2_{\rm{loc}}(M)\), where the latter is defined to be the class of predictable processes \(H\) such that there exists a localizing sequence \((\tau_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) for which \(H1_{(0,\tau_n]}\) is in \(L^2(M)\). 

\begin{definition}\label{def:L^p(M)_loc}
 For \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\), the space \(L_{\rm{loc}}^2(M)\) consists of predictable processes \(H\) for which there is  a sequence of stopping times \(\tau_n\nearrow\infty\) almost surely, such that 
    \begin{equation*}
        \mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\tau_n}H_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg]<\infty\quad\forall n\in\mathbb{N}.
    \end{equation*}
\end{definition}
In particular, we have that for \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\), \(H\in L^2_{\rm{loc}}(M)\) is equivalent to \(H1_{(0,\tau_n]}\in L^2(M)\) for each \(n\in\mathbb{N}\), and is equivalent to \(H\in L^2(M^{\tau_n})\) for each \(n\in\mathbb{N}\).
\bigbreak
The extension of Definition \ref{def:square-integrable stochastic integral} for the localized objects is then done by the usual "stitching together" procedure of localization. We define 
\begin{equation}\label{def:piece of local stochastic integral}
    I_M(H)^{(k)}\coloneqq H1_{(0,\tau_k]}\bullet X^{\tau_k}\quad \text{ on } (0,\tau_k] \text{ and } I_M(H)_0=0 ,
\end{equation}
where \((\tau_k)_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\) is a common localizing sequence of \(H\) and \(M\). We need to check that \eqref{def:piece of local stochastic integral} is consistent and well defined. For the following consideration, we restrict ourselves to \(\mathcal{N}^c\), where \(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{N})=0\) on which \(\lim_{k\to\infty}\tau_k=\infty\). We might choose \(\tau_k\leq\tau_{k-1}\) for every \(k\in\mathbb{N}\) on \(\mathcal{N}^c\). Consistency means, in this context,
\begin{equation*}
    I_M(H)^{(k)}=I_M(H)^{(k-1)}\text{ on } (0,\tau_{k-1}].
\end{equation*}
where \((0,\tau_{k-1}]\subseteq(0,\tau_{k}]\)
for every \(k\in\mathbb{N}\) as \(\tau_k\leq \tau_{k-1}\). To see this, recall the well-known property of the stochastic integral of Definition \ref{def:square-integrable stochastic integral}.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:stopping stochastic integrals}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\), \(\tau\) a stopping time and \(H\in L^2(M)\). Then
\begin{equation*}
    (H\bullet M)^{\tau}=H\bullet(M)^{\tau}=H1_{(0,\tau]}\bullet M.
\end{equation*}
\end{lemma}
Hence
\begin{equation*}
\big(I_M(H)^{(k)}_t\big)^{\tau_{k-1}}=\big(H1_{(0,\tau_k]}\bullet X^{\tau_k}\big)^{\tau_{k-1}}=H1_{(0,\tau_k]\cap(0,\tau_{k-1}]}\bullet X^{\tau_k}=I_M(H)^{(k-1)}_t.
\end{equation*}
which shows consistency. 



The well-definedness refers to the fact that one needs to check that \(H1_{(0,\tau_k]}\bullet X^{\tau_k}\) is well-defined in the sense of the integrals we understand at this point, that is, the integral of Definition \ref{def:square-integrable stochastic integral}. Concretely, we need to show that 
\begin{equation*}
    H1_{(0,\tau_k]}\in L^2(M^{\tau_k}).
\end{equation*} 
But this is obvious by Definition \ref{def:L^p(M)_loc}.
Furthermore, as \(\tau_k\nearrow\infty\), \((0,\tau_k]\nearrow\Omega/\mathcal{N}\times\mathbb{R}_+\), we can thus define the stochastic integral for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\) and \(H\in L^2_{\rm{loc}}(M)\) by stitching together the local construction \ref{def:piece of local stochastic integral}.
\begin{align}\label{def:local sotchastic integral}
    I_M(H)_t(\omega)=
    \begin{cases}
        \sum_{k\geq 1}I_M(H)^{(k)}_t(\omega)1_{(\tau_k(\omega)<t\leq\tau_{k+1}(\omega)]}\text{ for } t\in\mathbb{R}_+/\left\{0\right\}\\
        0 \text{ for } t=0
    \end{cases}
\end{align}
for \(\omega\in\mathcal{N}^c\). We might define \(I_M(H)\) on all of \(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\) by setting \(I_M(H)_t(\omega)=0\) for all \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\) and \(\omega\in\mathcal{N}\).
For a characterization of this integral analogous to Proposition \ref{prop:characterisation of stochastic integral} we refer to \parencite[Prop.~2.20]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}. 

\begin{remark}\label{rem:local integrator global integrand yields global integral}
The stochastic integral for the local objects, as defined in Definition \ref{def:local sotchastic integral}, is itself only a locally square-integrable local martingale. 
   For \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\) and \(M\in L^2(M)\) the respective stochastic integral is still in \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) \parencite[Thm.~2.5.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}.
\end{remark}

\subsection{Kunita-watanabe Decomposition}\label{sec:Kunita-Watanabe Decomposition}
Before turning towards stochastic integration with respect to classical semimartingales, we profit from the current familiarity of the reader with the square-integrable setup to introduce and state the celebrated Kunita Watanabe decomposition, which shall play a central role in the solution theory for the mean-variance hedging problem for square-integrable martingale price processes. For this, we will need a stronger notion of orthogonality on \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) than the one induced by the inner product structure. The fact that \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) is a space of uniformly integrable martingales leads to a richer geometry than the Hilbert space structure discussed in \eqref{eq:H^p identification}. 
\begin{definition}
    Let \(M,N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\). We say that \(M\) is strongly orthogonal to \(N\) and vise versa if their product \(MN\) is a martingale. In this case, we denote \(M\perp N\).
\end{definition}
In particular, for \(M\perp N\),
\begin{equation}
    (M,N)_{\mathcal{H}^2}=\mathbb{E}[M_{\infty}N_{\infty}]=\mathbb{E}[M_0N_0]=0.
\end{equation}
Hence, strong orthogonality implies the weak orthogonality of \(M,N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\). 
Furthermore, we have that \(M\perp N\iff \langle M,N\rangle=0\) \parencite[Rem.~11.2.5.]{CohenElliott2015StochasticCalculusApplications}.
\bigbreak
As explained in \cref{rem:if G_T closed then mean variance hedging is projection onto closed hilbert subspace}, in the context of mean-variance hedging, given a fixed price process \(X\), we are especially interested in studying the strategies for which the gains process evaluated at the final time is again square-integrable. In the square-integrable setting, we provided a partial answer affirming that for \(X\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\), strategies in \(L^2(M)\) fulfill this requirement. More precisely, we are interested in such strategies as they allow us to consider the mean-variance hedging results \eqref{eq:mean squared hedging} as a projection problem of the liability \(H\) onto the \(L^2\) subspace \(\mathbb{R}+G_\infty(\Theta)\), at least onto its closure if it is not closed itself. Alternatively, we can see this from a different angle. If the liability \(H\) were attainable, that is, if there existed \(\vartheta\in\Theta\) such that
\begin{equation}
    H=\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff X_t.
\end{equation}
This is a statement about the representability of \(H\in L^2(\mathcal{F}_T,\mathbb{P})\) as a stochastic integral with respect to \(X\). By assumption, we are in an incomplete market; hence, such a representation property can't be true in general. Nevertheless, we may ask whether we can separate the part of \(H\) that is representable by an integral with respect to \(X\) from the part that is not. The integrable part should be well distinguishable from the non-integral part. It is one of the fundamental insights of linear spaces that this is best done by decomposition into orthogonal parts. Obviously, this amounts to the same projection problem as previously discussed. In the square-integrable setting, the geometry of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) is richcan enough that we can provide complete theoretical answers to this question. The fundamental property is that the space of stochastic integrals with respect to square-integrable processes is closed in \(L^2\). This will give rise to the celebrated Kunita-Watanabe decomposition, which provides the representation theoretic solution to mean-square hedging for square-integrable martingale price processes. We need the following notion.
\begin{definition}\label{def:stable subspace}
    A \textit{stable} subspace of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) is a closed linear subspace \(\mathcal{A}\) of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) which is stable under stopping: For every \(M\in\mathcal{A}\) and every stopping time \(\tau\), the stopped process \(M^{\tau}\) is in \(\mathcal{A}\).
\end{definition}
The following example of a stable subspace constitutes the strong structural property of stochastic integrals in the square-integrable setting
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:stochastic integrals as stable subspace}
    For every \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) the space 
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal{I}^2(M)=\left\{\int H\diff M:H\in L^2(M)\right\}
    \end{equation}
    is a stable subspace of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). 
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    The closedness of \( \mathcal{I}^2(M)\) in \(\mathcal{H}^2_0\) follows directly from Theorem \ref{thm:Ito Isometry} as \( \mathcal{I}^2(M)\) is the isometric image under the map \(H\to H\bullet M\) of the Hilbert space \(L^2(M)\). Stability under stopping follows from Lemma \ref{lemma:stopping stochastic integrals}. Indeed, let \(\tau\) be a stopping time and \(N=H\bullet M\), the \(N^{\tau}=H1_{(0,\tau]}\bullet M\). As \(H\in L^2(M)\) it follows that \(H1_{(0,\tau]}\in L^2(M)\). Hence, \(N^{\tau}\in \mathcal{I}^2(M)\); thus, \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\) is stable under stopping. 
\end{proof}
Furthermore, we have that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:closedness of vartheta bullet M_T in L^2}
     G_{\infty}(L^2(M))=\left\{\int_0^{\infty} H\diff M:H\in L^2(M)\right\}
\end{equation}
is a closed subspace of \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\). Indeed, notice that under the identification discussed in \eqref{eq:H^p identification}
\begin{align*}
    &H\mapsto H_T\\
    &\mathcal{H}^2\to L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty}).
\end{align*} 
Theorem \ref{thm:Ito Isometry} reads as an isometry of Hilbert-spaces \(L^2(M)\to L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\). In particular, \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M))\) is the isometric image of the complete space \(L^2(M)\) in \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_T)\). The following shows the importance of a stable subspace in understanding the geometry of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) 
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:weak and strong stable subspaces}
    Suppose \(\mathcal{A}\) is a stable subspace of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) and denote by 
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal{A}^{\Delta}=\left\{N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2:(N,M)_{\mathcal{H}^2}=0\quad\forall M\in\mathcal{A}\right\}
    \end{equation}
    the weakly orthogonal complement of \(\mathcal{A}\) in \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). Then \(\mathcal{A}^{\Delta}\) is also strongly orthogonal to \(\mathcal{A}\) and a stable subspace of \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\). 
\end{proposition}
For the proof, we refer to \parencite[Prop.~5.5.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}
Note that the latter is conceptually astonishing. Weak orthogonality of two processes only states \(L^2\) orthogonality of their terminal values. It says nothing about the relation between the processes overall. Nevertheless, the weak orthogonal complement of stable subspaces is, in particular, strongly orthogonal, which is a property of processes. 
\begin{theorem}[Kunita-Watanabe Decomposition]\label{thm:KW}
    Fix \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\). Then every \(N\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) can be uniquely written as 
    \begin{equation}
        N = \int H\diff M +L
    \end{equation}
    for some \(H\in L^2(M)\) and some \(L\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) with \(L\perp M\). Furthermore, \(H=\frac{\diff \langle M,N\rangle}{\diff \langle M\rangle}\) is a predictable density of \(\langle M,N\rangle\) with respect to \(\langle M\rangle\). 
\end{theorem}
\begin{proof}
    Assume that \(M,N\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\). Let \(\hat{N}\) be the weakly orthogonal projection in \(\mathcal{H}_0^2\) onto the closed subspace \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\). Hence, there exists \(H\in L^2(M)\) such that \(\hat{N}=\int H\diff M\). Consider
    \begin{equation}
        L\coloneqq N-\hat{N}.
    \end{equation}
    \(L\) is weakly orthogonal to \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\) by construction. The latter is a stable subspace, hence by Proposition \ref{prop:weak and strong stable subspaces} \(L\) is also strongly orthogonal to \(\mathcal{I}^2(M)\) and in particular to \(M\) because the constant process \(1\) is in \(L^2(M)\) (this uses that \(\mathbb{P}_M\) is a finite measure if \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\)). In particular \(\langle M,N\rangle=0\). But then 
    \begin{equation}
        \langle M,N\rangle =\langle M,\int H\diff M\rangle+\langle M,L\rangle =\int H\diff \langle M,M\rangle.
    \end{equation}
    Therefore \(H=\frac{\diff \langle M,N\rangle}{\diff \langle M\rangle}\). Uniqueness follows from the uniqueness of the Hilbert projection. 
\end{proof}
We refer to \parencite[Cor.~5.7.]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus} for the proof.
\begin{corollary}\label{cor:random variable level Kunita Watanabe}
    Fix \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\). Then every random variable \(F\in L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\mathbb{P})\) can be uniquely written as 
    \begin{equation}
        F = \mathbb{E}[F|\mathcal{F}_0]+\int_0^{\infty}H_t\diff M_t+L_{\infty}
    \end{equation}
    for some \(H\in L^2(M)\) and some \(L\in \mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) such that \(L\perp M\). 
\end{corollary}
\begin{proof}
    We apply Theorem \ref{thm:KW} to the process \(V_t\coloneqq \mathbb
    E[F|\mathcal{F}_t]- E[F|\mathcal{F}_0]\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\) for \(t\in[0,T]\). We get 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:KW of V^F}
        V = V_0 + \int H\diff M+L.
    \end{equation}
    Noticing that \(F=\mathbb{E}[F|\mathcal{F}_{\infty}]\) and \(V_0=\mathbb{E}[F|\mathcal{F}_{0}]\) we obtain the claim by considering \eqref{eq:KW of V^F} at \(t=T\). 
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
Notice that if \(\mathcal{F}_0\) is trivial and completed, that is \(\mathcal{F}_0=\left\{\emptyset,\Omega\right\}\vee\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\), then \(\mathbb{E}[F|\mathcal{F}_0]=\mathbb{E}[F]\), \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost surely (with exact equality if \(\mathcal{F}_0=\left\{\emptyset,\Omega\right\}\)). 
\end{remark}

\subsection{Mean--variance hedging in square-integrable setting}\label{sec:Mean Variance Hedging in square-integrable setting}
We now have all the tools to discuss mean-variance hedging in the case where the price process is a square-integrable marginal. We restate the mean variance hedging problem in the square-integrable setting.
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:square-integrable mean variance via KW}
    Let \(H\in L^2(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\), \(X\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\) and \(L^2(X)\) defined as in Definition \ref{def:L^p(M)}. Furthermore, assume \(\mathcal{F}_0\) to be trivial.
    Consider the following minimization problem
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:square-integrable mean square hedging}
        \inf_{(V_0,\vartheta)\in \mathbb{R}\times L^2(X)}\lVert V_0+\int_{0}^{\infty}\vartheta\diff X-H\rVert_{L^2}.
    \end{equation}
    Then the infimum is attained in \(\mathbb{R}+G_{\infty}(L^2(X))\) and the optimal strategy \((V_0^*,\vartheta^*)\) is given by \((\mathbb{E}[H],\vartheta^H)\), where the latter are provided by the Kunita Watanabe decomposition of \(H\):
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:KW of H}
        H = \mathbb{E}[H]+\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta^H\diff X+L_{\infty}.
    \end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    Existence essentially follows from \eqref{eq:closedness of vartheta bullet M_T in L^2}. In particular, we have that  
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal{A}(L^2(X))=\mathbb{R}+G_{\infty}(L^2(X))\subseteq L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty}),
    \end{equation}
    is closed. Hence existence follows from the Hilbert projection theorem, that is the existence of the decomposition
    \begin{equation}
        L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})=\mathcal{A}(L^2(X))\oplus(\mathcal{A}(L^2(X)))^{\perp}.
    \end{equation}
    where \((\cdot)^{\perp}\) denotes the weak orthogonal complement in \(L^2\).
    \bigbreak
    In order to show that the solution is given by the Kunita Watanabe decomposition, recall the random variable version \cref{cor:random variable level Kunita Watanabe} which followed from Theorem \ref{thm:KW} by identifying the random variable \(H\) with the uniformly integrable martingale  
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:identifying r.v. H with process V}
        V^H_t\coloneqq \mathbb{E}[H|\mathcal{F}_t]\quad t\in[0,T].\footnotemark
    \end{equation}
 In particular, \(H=V^H_{\infty}\). The Kunita Watanabe decomposition of \(H\) with respect to \(X\) is given by 
 \begin{equation}\label{eq:KW of liability H}
    H=\mathbb{E}[H]+\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta^H\diff X+L_{\infty} ,
 \end{equation}
 where \(L\perp X\). 
 \\\\
 Recall from the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:KW} that \(L\) is in \((\mathcal{I}(X))^{\perp}\) by construction. This is exactly the statement that \(L_{\infty}\) is weakly orthogonal to all terminal values of integral processes in \(\mathcal{I}^2(X)\), that is, to all elements of \(G_{\infty}(L^2(X))\). Moreover, it is the (unique) orthogonal projection of \(H\) onto the \(L^2\)-closed subspace \(\mathcal{A}(L^2(X))\). Indeed
\begin{align*}
    &(L_{\infty},V_0+\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta^Z\diff X)_{L^2}= (L_{\infty},V_0)_{L^2}+ (L_{\infty},\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta^H\diff X)_{L^2}=V_0\mathbb{E}[L_{\infty}]=0,
\end{align*}
for all \(V_0\in\mathbb{R}\) and for all \(\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta^H\diff X\in G_{\infty}(L^2(X))\). The second equality follows because \(L_{\infty}\) is orthogonal to all elements in \(G_{\infty}(L^2(X))\), and the last equality follows because \(L\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\) implies that \(L_{\infty}\) exists and \(\mathbb{E}[L_{\infty}]=\mathbb{E}[L_0]=0\). Hence, \eqref{eq:KW of liability H} is the orthogonal decomposition of \(H\) onto \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})=\mathcal{A}(L^2(X))\oplus \mathcal{A}(L^2(X))^{\perp}\), and the claim follows by pythagoras
\begin{equation}
   \lVert Z-H\rVert_{L^2}=\lVert Z -\left(\mathbb{E}[H]+\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta^H\diff X\right)\rVert_{L^2}+\lVert L_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2}\geq \lVert L_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2}=\lVert \mathbb{E}[H]+\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta^H\diff X-H\rVert_{L^2},
\end{equation}
for any \(Z\in\mathcal{A}(L^2(X))\). Taking \(\inf\) over such \(Z\) yields the claim. This shows that \(\mathbb{E}[H]+\int \vartheta^H\diff X\) is the minimizer in \(\mathbb{R}+G_{\infty}(L^2(X))\).
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
 For self-consistency and simplicity, we formulated the result for \(X\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2\). Nevertheless, it should be noted that all these results can be localized. Following the presentation in  \parencite{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}and \cref{rem:local integrator global integrand yields global integral}, one can assume \(X\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\) everywhere. In particular, we then extend the definition of strong orthogonality to  \(X\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\) by saying that \(M,N\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\) are strongly orthogonal if \(MN\) is a local martingale. 
\end{remark}

\subsection{Stochastic integration with respect to semimartingales}\label{sec:stochastic integration with respect to semimartingales}
In light of the discussion of mean-variance hedging for  continuous semimartingale price processes, the discussion of Section \ref{sec:algorithmically generated random variables} and Section \ref{sec:extension of deep mean squared hedging to semimartingales} requires some elementary results of more general stochastic integration, which we present here. The approach is heavily based on \parencite{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}, and we refer to the latter for more details, missing proofs, and the construction of the stochastic integral in full generality.
As mentionend in the introductory discussion around \eqref{eq:heuristic integral with respect to semimartingale} the construction of the stochastic integral with respect to square-integrable martingales constitutes the conceptual cornerstone for the development towards stochastic integration with respect to semimartingales. Nevertheless we present a different approach, due to \parencite{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}, which is independently insightfull and yields some results, need in Section \ref{sec:algorithmically generated random variables}.
\\\\
For the interested reader, it shall be noted that the notion of semimartingales used in \parencite[Chapter.~2]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} is seemingly different from the classical one used in Definition \ref{def:semimartingale} at first sight. 

In \parencite[Chapter.~4]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} semimartingales are defined to be processes for which the integration of simple predictable processes is continuous under uniform convergence. The latter guaranties a notion of bounded convergence for the respective integral, which is given a central role in the construction of a \textit{sensible} notion of an integral. It is the celebrated Bichteler-Dellacherie Theorem \parencite[Thm.~47]{DellacherieMeyer1982ProbabilitiesPotentialB} that makes the junction between these two notions. 
\bigbreak
A key conceptual difference in this approach is that we need to enlarge the definition of elementary predictable processes from Definition \ref{def:elementary predictable process} to the notion of \textit{simple predictable processes}. 
\begin{definition}\label{def:simple predictable process}
    We call \(\vartheta\) an \textit{simple predictable process} if there exists a finite, increasing sequence of stopping times, \(0=\tau_0<\dots<\tau_{n+1}<\infty\) and a sequence of bounded random variables \((\vartheta^i)_{i=0}^n\subseteq\mathcal{L}^{\infty}(\mathcal{F}_{\tau_i},\mathbb{P})\) such that 
    \begin{equation}
        \vartheta = \vartheta^01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{i=1}^n\vartheta^i1_{(\tau_i,\tau_{i+1}]}.
    \end{equation}
In particular, we have that \(\vartheta^i\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{\tau_i}\)-measurable. We denote the set of elementary predictable processes by \(\mathcal{S}\) and we say \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{S}\) is associated with a sequence \((\tau_k)_{k=0}^{n+1}\).
\end{definition}
Clearly \(\mathcal{E}\subseteq\mathcal{S}\). We further denote \(\mathcal{S}_{ucp}\) the set \(\mathcal{S}\) endowed with the topology of \(ucp\)-convergence. Furthermore, we denote by \(\mathbb{L}\) the class of adapted càdlàg processes, \(b\mathbb{L}\) the respective bounded subset, and by \(\mathbb{D}\) the class of adapted càdlàg processes. We will construct the stochastic integral \(\int \vartheta\diff X\) for \(\vartheta\in \mathbb{L}\) and \(X\) a classical semimartingale. The idea is similar to our previous construction in that we first define the stochastic integral for simple processes before extending it by density to a larger class of integrable processes. This necessitates the notion of continuity, which is a topology. In this case, the adapted notion is \(ucp\)
-continuity. 
\begin{definition}\label{def:ucp convergence}
    Let \((X^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) be real-valued stochastic processes. We say \(X^n\to X\) converges \textit{uniformly on compact sets} (\(ucp\)) if 
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{P}((X^n-X)^*_t>\epsilon)=0\;\forall\epsilon>0\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+,
    \end{equation}
    where \((X^n-X)^*_t=\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|X^n_t-X_t|\) and  \((X^n-X)^*_{\infty}=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}|X^n_t-X_t|\). 
\end{definition}
Put differently, \(X^n\overset{ucp}{\to}X\), if \((X^n-X)^*_t\) converges to \(0\) in probability for all \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\). 
Clearly, if \((X^n-X)_{\infty}^*\to 0\) converges in probability, then \(X^n\overset{ucp}{\to}X\). As for the convergence in probability of random variables, the corresponding topology is induced by the following metric
\begin{equation}
    d(X,Y)=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\frac{1}{2^n}\mathbb{E}[1\wedge(X-Y)^*_n].
\end{equation}
The corresponding space \(\mathbb{D}_{ucp}\), equipped with the proposed metric, is complete. For later reference, we also introduce \(p-ucp\) convergence
\begin{definition}\label{def:p-ucp convergence}
    Let \((X^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) be real-valued stochastic processes and \(p\in[1,\infty)\). We say \(X^n\to X\) converges in \(p-ucp\) if 
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}[|(X^n-X)^*_t|{^p}]=0\;\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+,
    \end{equation} 
\end{definition}
The idea is very similar to the \(ucp\)-convergence, in the sense that we generalize the usual \(L^p\)-convergence of random variables to stochastic processes by requiring the \(L^p\)-convergence to be uniform in time. As \(L^p\) convergence implies convergence in probability \parencite[Prop.~10.3]{LeGall2022MeasureTheoryProbabilityStochasticProcesses}, it is immediate that \(p-ucp\) convergence implies \(ucp\) convergence. 
\\\\
The stochastic integral for the process Definition \ref{def:simple predictable process} is naturally defined as follows
\begin{equation}\label{def:simple integration}
    I_X(\vartheta)=\vartheta^0X_0+\sum_{i=1}^n \vartheta^i(X_{\tau_{i+1}}-X_{\tau_i}).
\end{equation}
The following result constitutes the key observation 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:ucp continuity of integral map}
    I_X:\mathcal{S}_{ucp}\to\mathbb{D}_{ucp}
\end{equation}
is continuous with respect to the \(ucp\)-topology. Furthermore, one shows that \(\mathcal{S}_{ucp}\) is dense in \(\mathbb{L}\) for the \(ucp\)-topology. It therefore follows that there exists a unique continuous extension 
\begin{equation}
    \tilde{I}_X:\mathbb{L}_{ucp}\to\mathbb{D}_{ucp}
\end{equation}
 For the proof of continuity of the map \eqref{eq:ucp continuity of integral map}, we refer to \parencite[Thm.~11]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}. For the density of \(\mathcal{S}_{ucp}\) in \(\mathbb{L}\), we reproduce the proof given in \parencite[Thm.~10,Ch.II]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} as it forms an interesting contrast to the monotone class argument used in Proposition \ref{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class}. 
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:ucp density of simple process in cadlag processes}
    The space \(\mathcal{S}\) is dense in \(\mathbb{L}\) for the \(ucp\)-topology
\end{proposition}
Before going into details, we provide some conceptual ideas about the proof strategy. Given some \(Y\in\mathbb{L}\), we want to approximate it by elements of \(\mathcal{S}\) in the \(ucp\)-sense. After arguing that w.l.o.g. we can take \(Y\in b\mathbb{L}\), the idea is to approximate every path of \(Y\) by step functions that are uniformly \(\epsilon\)-close. We want to do this for every random path of \(Y\); thus, we have to construct these step functions by cutting up \(Y\) whenever \(Y\) leaves a uniform \(\epsilon\)-tube. As this is a random event, we do this by stopping times. This will yield step functions that are \(\epsilon\) close in every \((\omega,t)\). \\\\
Nota bene, this yields an approximating sequence in \(\mathcal{S}\) and not in \(\mathcal{E}\) in general. It then remains to check that the approximating sequence in \(\mathcal{S}\) indeed approximates \(Y\) in \(ucp\). The main technical difficulties come from the sequence of stopping times defined for the uniform approximation; they should increase to \(\infty\) in order to form a partition of \(\mathbb{R}_+\). For this, we shall need to take the right-continuous limit process of \(Y\). By constructing the approximating step functions carefully, we still approximate our original process \(Y\).
\begin{proof}
 First, notice that for \(Y\in\mathbb{L}\), we can stop \(Y\) with the stopping times 
    \begin{equation}
        T_n=\inf\left\{t:|Y_t|>n]\right\}
    \end{equation}
    Because \(Y\) is left-continuous by assumption, \(Y^{T_n}1_{T_n>0}\) is bounded by \(n\); in particular, \(Y^n\coloneqq Y^{T_n}1_{T_n>0}\in b\mathbb{L}\). Notice that the multiplication by \(1_{T_n>0}\) is necessary as we might have \(Y_0(\omega)=n+1\), then \(T_n=0\) and \(Y^{T_n}=Y_0>n\). Now,
    \begin{equation}
        Y_n\overset{ucp}{\to}Y
    \end{equation}
 Indeed,
    \begin{equation}
        \left\{\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|Y_t^n-Y_t|>\epsilon\right\}\subseteq \left\{T_n\leq t\right\}
    \end{equation}
    for every \(\epsilon>0\) and
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{P}(T_n\leq t)\leq\mathbb{P}(Y_t^*>n)
    \end{equation}
    and the right-hand side tends to \(0\) for every \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\). Hence, \(b\mathbb{L}\) is dense in \(\mathbb{L}\) for the \(ucp\)-topology. Hence, without loss of generality, assume \(Y\in b\mathbb{L}\). The idea is to approximate \(Z\) by a step-wise constant process in a uniform \(\epsilon\)-tube for every random path. We do this as follows. Let 
    \(Z_t\coloneqq Y_t^{+}=\lim_{s\to t, s>t}Y_t\). Define the following stopping times for fixed \(\epsilon>0\)
    \begin{align*}
        &T_0^{\epsilon}=0\\
        &T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}=\inf\left\{t:t>T_{n}^{\epsilon},\;|Z _{T_{n}^{\epsilon}}-Z_t|>\epsilon\right\}.
    \end{align*}
    Since \(Z\) is càdlàg, the \(T_n^{\epsilon}\) are stopping times strictly increasing to \(\infty\) \footnote{It is crucial to define the random times \(T_n^{\epsilon}\) for a right-continuous process \(Z\). Indeed, if \(Z\) was not right-continuous, \(T_n\) are typically equal to \(T_1\) for all \(n\geq 1\). Hence, consider the cadlag process \(Z=Y^{-}\) instead of the caglad process \(Y\) as crucial.}.
 Hence, we can build the uniform \(\epsilon\)-approximants
    \begin{equation}
        Z^{\epsilon}=\sum_{n\geq 0} Z_{T_n^{\epsilon}}1_{[T_{n}^{\epsilon},T_{n+1}^{\epsilon})}.
    \end{equation}
 By construction, \(Z^{\epsilon}\) tends uniformly to \(Z\) as \(\epsilon\) tends to \(0\). Indeed, for all \(\omega,t\in\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\) we have 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:epsilon approximants of cadlag Z}
        |Z_t(\omega)-Z_t^{\epsilon}(\omega)|\leq\sum_{n\geq 0}|Z_{t}(\omega)-Z_{T_n^{\epsilon}((\omega))}(\omega)|1_{[T_{n}^{\epsilon}(\omega),T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}(\omega))}(t)\leq\epsilon
    \end{equation}
    because 
    \begin{equation}
        |Z_{t}(\omega)-Z_{T_n^{\epsilon}((\omega))}(\omega)|\leq\epsilon,\quad t\in[T_{n}^{\epsilon}(\omega),T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}(\omega)).
    \end{equation}
 Indeed, for \(t<T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}\) this is true, because if not, and assuming w.l.o.g. that \(t>T_{n}^{\epsilon}\), this \(t\) is such that \(|Z_{t}(\omega)-Z_{T_n^{\epsilon}((\omega))}(\omega)|>\epsilon\) and \(T_{n}^{\epsilon}<t<T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}\) contradict the definition of \(T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}\). Note that this is not true for the interval \((T_{n}^{\epsilon}(\omega),T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}(\omega)]\) as 
     \begin{equation}
        |Z_{T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}}-Z_{T_n^{\epsilon}}|
    \end{equation}
    is generally not  bounded from above. Indeed, we have the upper bound \(|X_{T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}}-X_{T_n^{\epsilon}}|\leq\epsilon+\Delta |X_{T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}}|\) but \(|\Delta X_{T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}}|\) can take any finite value. 
    But what is true, indeed, is that 
  \begin{equation}
        |Z_{t-}(\omega)-Z_{T_n^{\epsilon}((\omega))}(\omega)|\leq\epsilon,\quad t\in(T_{n}^{\epsilon}(\omega),T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}(\omega)].
    \end{equation}
    where \(Z_{t-}\) denotes the left-limit process of \(Z\) at \(t\). Indeed,
    \begin{equation}
      |Z_{T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}((\omega))-}(\omega)-Z_{T_n^{\epsilon}((\omega))}(\omega)|  \leq \epsilon
    \end{equation}
    because \(\Delta Z_{T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}((\omega))-}=0\). Hence, let 
    \begin{equation}
        U^{\epsilon}=1_{\left\{0\right\}}Y_0+\sum_{n\geq 1} Z_{T_n^{\epsilon}}1_{(T_{n}^{\epsilon},T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}]} 
    \end{equation}
    By our previous consideration, it is clear that \(U_\epsilon\) converges uniformly to \(1_{\left\{0\right\}}Y_0+Z_{-}=Y\) as \(\epsilon\to 0\). It is clear \(U^{\epsilon}\in b\mathbb{L}\). Finally, define 
    \begin{equation}
        Y^{n,\epsilon}=1_{\left\{0\right\}}Y_0+\sum_{k\geq 0}Z_{T_k^{\epsilon}}1_{(T_{k}^{\epsilon},T_{k+1}^{\epsilon}]}.
    \end{equation}
    By taking \(\epsilon\) small enough and \(n\) large enough, \(Y_{n,\epsilon}\) can be made arbitrarily close to \(Y\) (i.e., as a function on \(\Omega\times \mathbb{R}_+\)). Hence, by a diagonal argument, there exists a common sequence such that \(Y^{n,\epsilon(n)}\) converges to \(Y\) uniformly on compacts.  Clearly \(Y^{n,\epsilon}\in\mathcal{S}\) for every \(\epsilon>0\) and \(n\in\mathbb{N}\).
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
Observe two things. A monotone class argument as in Proposition \ref{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class} would not be well adapted, as it deeply relies on a class closed under bounded, monotone, pointwise convergence, which is ill conditioned for making statements about convergence in \(ucp\). More precisely, \(ucp\) convergence is too strong in the sense that it is generally not implied by uniformly bounded monotone convergence; hence, a class \begin{equation}
    \mathcal{H}_{ucp}=\left\{ U \in \mathcal{A}:\forall \epsilon\;\exists H\in\mathcal{S}:\sup_{0\leq s\leq t}|H_t-U_t|<\epsilon, \mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.} \forall t\in\mathbb{R}+\right\}
\end{equation} 
is not closed under bounded monotone limits, as this is a pointwise property and doesn't conserve a uniform bound (not in probability or almost surely). \\\\
Secondly, the proof idea above crucially relies on partitioning the positive real line into random intervals \((\cdot]\) or \([\cdot)\) in a way that allows us to approximate \(Y,Y_+,Y_-\) uniformly on such a random subinterval. We have \(\bigcup_{n\in\mathbb{N}} (T_n^{\epsilon},T_{n+1}^{\epsilon}]=\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\). Partitioning \(\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\) in this way can only be done by stopping times; thus, the above argument cannot be reduced to a proof for \(\mathcal{E}\) instead of \(\mathcal{S}\).
\end{remark}
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:ucp continuity of general stochastic integral}
    Let \(X\) be a semimartingale. Then the mapping 
    \begin{equation}
        I_X:\mathcal{S}_{ucp}\to\mathbb{D}_{ucp} 
    \end{equation}
    is continuous. 
\end{theorem}
This allows us to define
\begin{definition}\label{def:stochastic integral w.r.t. semimartingales}
    Let \(X\) be a semimartingale. We define the stochastic integral
    \begin{align*}
        I_X:\;&\mathbb{L}\to\mathbb{D}\\
        &\vartheta\mapsto\int \vartheta\diff X
    \end{align*}
    as the unique continuous extension of the map in Theorem \ref{thm:ucp continuity of general stochastic integral} from \(\mathcal{S}\) to \(\overline{S}^{ucp}=\mathbb{L}_{ucp}\).  
\end{definition}
One can significantly enlarge the space of integrands to predictable processes \parencite[Ch.IV]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}. The construction is more involved. Broadly speaking, in order to allow for generally predictable integrands, one first restricts the integrators to semimartingales in \(X\in \mathcal{S}^2\), where \(\mathcal{S}^2\) contains special semimartingales \(X\) with finite \(\mathcal{S}^2\)-norm
\begin{equation}
    \lVert X\rVert_{\mathcal{S}^2}=\lVert [M]_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}\rVert_{L^2}+\lVert \int_0^{\infty}\diff |A|_t\rVert_{L^2},
\end{equation}
where \(X=X_0+M+A\) denotes the canonical decomposition of \(X\) and \(|A|\) denotes the variation process of \(A\). Due to \eqref{eq:P_M is finite measure}, \(X=X_0+M+A\in\mathcal{S}^2\) if and only if \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) and \(A\) have square-integrable variation.
If \(\vartheta\bullet X\) exists, it has a canonical decomposition \(\vartheta\bullet X = \vartheta\bullet M+\vartheta\bullet A\). Indeed, it follows from \parencite[Thm.~17,Ch.IV]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} that for \(A\) of finite variation and predictable, one can show that \(\int \vartheta\diff A\) is again predictable. Furthermore, we have
\begin{equation}
    \lVert\int \vartheta\diff X\rVert_{\mathcal{S}^2}=\lVert\left(\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff [M]_t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\rVert_{L^2}+\lVert \int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta_t|\diff |A|_t\rVert_{L^2}\,.
\end{equation}
We define the class of \(X\)-integrable processes as the class of predictable processes \(\vartheta\) such that
\begin{equation}\label{eq:X integrable processes for X in S^2}
\lVert\left(\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff [M]_t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\rVert_{L^2}+\lVert \int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta_t|\diff |A|_t\rVert_{L^2}<\infty.
\end{equation}

Notice that the first term on the right-hand side corresponds exactly to \(\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}\).
For later reference, this space will play a central role in Section \ref{sec:extension of deep mean squared hedging to semimartingales} and will be denoted by \(L^2(M,A)\). It thus follows that for \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2\) with canonical decomposition \(X=X_0+M+A\) and \(\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)\), we have that \(\int \vartheta\diff X\) exists and 
\begin{equation}
    \int \vartheta\diff X = \int \vartheta\diff M+\int \vartheta\diff A,
\end{equation}
is the canonical decomposition of \(\int \vartheta\diff X\) and \(\int \vartheta\diff X\in\mathcal{S}^2\). By the definition of \(L^2(M,A)\) the same conclusion holds if \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) that is \(X=X_0+M+A\) with \(M\in\mathcal{H}_{0,\rm{loc}}^2\) and 
\(A\) predictable and locally square-integrable.
 This is the most general notion of stochastic integration we shall use in this thesis. We set the standing assumption. 
\begin{align*}
    \text{All semimartingales in this thesis are taken to be in \(\mathcal{S}_{\rm{loc}}^2\)},
\end{align*}
where \(\mathcal{S}_{\rm{loc}}^2\) consists of special semimartingales with canonical decomposition \(X=X_0+M+A\), where \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\) and \(A\) are predictable, locally square-integrable variations, that is there exists a localizing sequence \((\tau_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) such that \(|A|^*_{\infty}=\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}|A_t|\in L^2(\mathbb{P}\)). In particular, this implies that \(\langle M\rangle\) exists. Hence, the relevant notion of integrability in this thesis is given by the following definition.
\begin{definition}\label{def:X integrable processes for X in S^2_loc}
    Let \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) with canonical decomposition \(X=X_0+M+A\). Then we call a process \(\vartheta\) \(X\)-integrable if it is predictable and
    \begin{equation}
        \lVert\left(\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff [M]_t\right)^{\frac{1}{2}}\rVert_{L^2}+\lVert \int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta_t|\diff |A|_t\rVert_{L^2}<\infty
    \end{equation}
    We denote the set of \(X\)-integrable processes for \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) by \(L(\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}})\). 
\end{definition}
\bigbreak
Nevertheless, we mention that one can define a more general notion of stochastic integration. 
The crucial observation is then to notice that any semimartingale is \newline (pre-)locally in \(\mathcal{S}^2\). Hence, we can extend the integral by localization to any semimartingale \(X\). For a classical semimartingale \(X\), we denote the set of \(X\)-integrable processes by \(L(X)\). We define \(L(X)\) to be the set of predictable processes for which the integral in the definition on the bottom of page 165 of \parencite{Protter2005StochasticIntegration} is well defined. For practical purposes, we mostly use the fact that locally bounded, predictable processes are in \(L(X)\), as shown in \parencite[Thm.~15,Ch.IV]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}.
\bigbreak
Regarding the development of Section \ref{sec:Mean-variance Hedging with continuous price process} and the following sections, it will be advantageous to allow \(X\) to be only locally in \(\mathcal{S}^2\), that is 


\section{Mean-variance hedging in the semimartingale case}\label{sec:Mean-variance Hedging with continuous price process}

We now have sufficient knowledge of stochastic integration to consider the mean-variance hedging problem when
the discounted price process \(X\) is essentially allowed to have a finite variation part adding to the square-integrable martingale part as previously studied in Section \ref{sec:Mean Variance Hedging in square-integrable setting}. This is
the natural situation under the statistical measure \(\mathbb{P}\), under which the discounted asset price need not be a
martingale. Consequently, the Hilbert space argument for \(\mathcal{H}^2\) from
Section \ref{sec:Mean Variance Hedging in square-integrable setting} no longer applies.
 For the following, we essentially follow \parencite{Schweizer2001GuidedTourQuadraticHedging}. We will not give proofs for the following results as they are all nicely presented in \parencite[Chapter.~4]{Schweizer2001GuidedTourQuadraticHedging}. Recall the standing assumption that \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\). 
 \bigbreak
We recall the problem at hand. 
Let \(H \in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) be a contingent claim. For a suitable class \(\Theta\subseteq\Theta^{(2)}\) of 
square-integrable strategies, the mean-variance hedging problem is
\begin{equation}\label{eq:MV hedging restatement}
        \inf_{V_0 \in \mathbb R,\ \vartheta \in \Theta}
        \mathbb{E}\bigg[
        \left(
        V_0 + \int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t \diff X_t -H
        \right)^2
        \bigg].
\end{equation}
Thus, one wants to approximate \(H\) in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\) by terminal values of self-financing
strategies.
\[
        V_0 + G_{\infty}(\vartheta)
        =
        V_0 + \int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t\,dX_t .
\]
Equivalently, the problem is to project the random variable \(H\) onto the affine space
\[
        \mathbb R + G_{\infty}(\Theta),
        \qquad
        G_{\infty}(\Theta)
        :=
        \left\{
        \int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t\,dX_t : \vartheta \in \Theta
        \right\}.
\]
In the square-integrable martingale case, it was a consequece of the Itô-isometry that the space \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\subseteq L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\mathbb{P})\) is closed, showing the existence of the mean-variance hedging problem. Even more, the Kunita Watanabe decomposition, relying on the geometric properties of \(\mathcal{H}^2\) and the fact that \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) is not only closed in \(L^2\) but is even a stable subspace of the latter, allowed us to characterize the solution to the mean-variance hedging problem explicitly. Fundamentally, the results rely on the property of the stochastic integral that for \( X\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2,\;\vartheta\in L^2(M)\) we have 
\begin{equation}
   \int \vartheta\diff X\in\mathcal{H}_{0}^2.
\end{equation}
This is not true in general. For local martingales X and locally bounded, predictable integrands \(\vartheta\), the integral is a local martingale \parencite[Ch. IV, Thm.~29]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}. In the most general case, that is for a semimartingale \(X\) and  \(X\)-integrable \(\vartheta\), the resulting stochastic integral needs to be a local martingale (see Emery's example after \parencite[Thm.~34]{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}). Hence, the study of the mean-variance problem for more general price processes is more involved. The following discussion largely follows the overview \parencite{Schweizer2001GuidedTourQuadraticHedging}. We assume \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) and refer to this setting as the semimartingale case. 
\bigbreak
In the semimartingale case, we would like to proceed similarly to the square-integrable martingale case by reducing the mean-variance hedging problem to a projection problem in \(L^2\). As observed in \cref{rem:if G_T closed then mean variance hedging is projection onto closed hilbert subspace}, we can do so if \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) is closed in \(L^2(P)\). Thus, we are very much interested in the study of sufficient conditions for which \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P})\) is closed. 


A natural first step is to consider the mean-variance hedging problem on the larger space 
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{R}+\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2},
\end{equation}
which is a \(L^2\)-closed subspace by Lemma \ref{lemma:A closed if G closed}. In particular, the mean-variance solution \((V_0^*,g^*)\) exists in \(\mathbb{R}+\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\) and is unique as element of the latter by the Hilbert projection theorem. Notice two things. The mean-variance solution is unique as an element \(V_0^*+g^*\in\mathbb{R}+\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\) and not necessarily as a pair  \((V_0^*,g^*)\). In particular, the mean-variance price \(V_0^*\) is not unique. This is easily seen from the fact that if  \((V_0^*,g^*)\) is mean-variance solution, so is \((\widetilde{V_0}^*,\widetilde{g}^*)\) with \(\widetilde{V_0}^*=\widetilde{V_0}^*+a\), \(\widetilde{g}^*=\widetilde{g}^*-a\) with \(a\in\mathbb{R}/\left\{0\right\}\), as 
\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{V_0}^*+\widetilde{g}^*=V_0^*+a+g^*-a=V_0^*+g^*.
\end{equation}
One might wonder whether this is all that can go wrong regarding the uniqueness in \(\mathbb{R}\times\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\). Indeed, assume \(\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\) does not contain any non-zeroro constants. As \(\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\) is itself a linear subspace, this is equivalent to requiring that it does not contain the payoff equal to \(1\). Assume  \((V_0^*,g^*)\) and \((\widetilde{V_0}^*,\widetilde{g}^*)\), where both are mean-variance portfolios. Then
\begin{align*}
    V_0^*-\widetilde{V_0}^*=\widetilde{g}^*-g^*\in\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}.
\end{align*}
But \(V_0^*-\widetilde{V_0}^*\) is a constant random variable, which by assumption is not an element of \(\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\) except if it is zero. Hence \(V_0^*=\widetilde{V_0}^*\) and 
\(g^*=\widetilde{g}^*\). The condition that \(\overline{G_{\infty}(\Theta)}^{L^2}\) does not contain any non-zero constant random variables even allows for an explicit formula for the mean-variance price, as we shall see in Lemma \ref{lemma:no approximate profits}. Observe that the uniqueness argument also shows that if the mean-variance solution exists in \((V_0^*,g^*)\in\mathbb{R}\times G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) and \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) does not contain non zero constants, then \((V_0^*,g^*)\in\mathbb{R}\times G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) is unique. The reason, why the no-approximate profit condition Definition \ref{def:no approximate profits} is formulated for the \(L^2\) closure of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) is due to the first equivalence in Lemma \ref{lemma:no approximate profits}. 



In accordance with \parencite{Schweizer2001GuidedTourQuadraticHedging}, the following developments will be formulated for a general linear subspace \(\mathcal{G}\subseteq L^2(P)\) and will accordingly define \(\mathcal{A}=\mathbb{R}+\mathcal{G}\). The reader might think of \(\mathcal{G}=G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) for some set of admissible strategies \(\Theta\subseteq\Theta^{(2)}\). In the following, all the closures are taken with respect to the \(L^2\)-topology.
\begin{definition}\label{def:no approximate profits}
    We say that \(\mathcal{G}\) admits \textit{no approximate profits} in \(L^2\) if \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}\) does \textbf{not} contain the constant \(1\). 
\end{definition}
This is a no-arbitrage condition. Indeed, if \(\mathcal{G}\) admits no approximate profits, one cannot approximate the riskless payoff \(V_{\infty}=1\) by a self-financing strategy with initial wealth \(V_0=0\) in \(\mathcal{G}\). As \(\mathcal{G}\) is a linear subspace of \(L^2\) if \(1\notin\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{L^2}\), \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{L^2}\) does not contain any constant random variable. Furthermore, alignment with the general insight of mathematical finance that the existence of a local martingale measure and no-arbitrage-like conditions are intimately related, the no approximate profit condition can be formulated as an existence statement of an absolutely continuous measure.
\begin{definition}
A \emph{signed $\mathcal{G}$-martingale measure} is a signed measure $\mathbb{Q}$ on
$(\Omega,\mathcal{F})$ with $\mathbb{Q}[\Omega]=1$, $\mathbb{Q}\ll \mathbb{P}$ with $\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}\in L^2(\mathbb{P})$, and
\[
\mathbb{E}_\mathbb{Q}[g] \;=\; \mathbb{E}\!\bigg[\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}\,g\bigg] \;=\; 0
\qquad\text{for all } g\in\mathcal{G}.
\]
$\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})$ denotes the convex set of all signed
$\mathcal{G}$-martingale measures, and an element $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}$
of $\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})$ is called \emph{variance-optimal} if it minimizes
\[
\left\|\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}\right\|_{L^2}
\;=\;
\sqrt{1+\operatorname{Var}\!\left[\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}\right]}
\]
 over all  $\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})$.
\end{definition}
Notice that we only require \(\mathbb{Q}\) to be a signed measure.
\bigbreak
The following lemma justifies the former definition
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:no approximate profits}
Let $\mathcal{G}$ be a linear subspace of $L^2$. Then:
\begin{enumerate}
\item $\mathcal{G}$ admits no approximate profits in $L^2$ if and only if
$\mathbb{P}^2_s(\mathcal{G})\neq\emptyset$.

\item If $\mathcal{G}$ admits no approximate profits in $L^2$, then
$\bar{\mathcal{A}}=\mathbb{R}+\bar{\mathcal{G}}$.

\item If $\mathcal{G}$ admits no approximate profits in $L^2$, then the
variance-optimal signed $\mathcal{G}$-martingale measure $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}$
exists, is unique, and satisfies
\begin{equation}
\frac{d\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}}{d\mathbb{P}}\in\bar{\mathcal{A}}.
\end{equation}
\end{enumerate}
\end{lemma}
For completeness we reproduce the proof given in \parencite[Lemma.~4.1.]{Schweizer2001GuidedTourQuadraticHedging}.
For this we need to lemmata
\begin{lemma}
The set 
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{Z}=\left\{\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}:\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}^2_t(\mathcal{G})\right\},
\end{equation}
is closed in \(L^2\) and convex. 
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
By definitinon of \(\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}^2_t(\mathcal{G})\), \(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\). Furthermore, 
    every measurable function \(Z\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) such that \(\mathbb{E}[Z]=1\) induces a signed measure \(\mathbb{Q}(A)=\mathbb{E}[1_AZ]\) hence we have 
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{Z}=\mathcal{G}^{\perp}\cap \ker(\phi),
\end{equation}
where \(\mathcal{G}^{\perp}\) denots the orthogonal complement of \(\mathcal{G}\) in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\) and \(\phi:Z\mapsto\mathbb{E}[Z]-1\) is a continuous map from \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\) to \(\mathbb{R}\). Hence, \(\mathcal{Z}\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P})\) is closed. Regarding convexity, it clear that for \(\lambda\in [0,1]\)
\begin{equation}
    \lambda\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}_1}{\diff \mathbb{P}}+(1-\lambda)\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}_2}{\diff \mathbb{P}}=\frac{\diff \widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}
\end{equation}
with \(\widetilde{Q}=\lambda\mathbb{Q}_1+(1-\lambda)\mathbb{Q}_2\in \mathbb{\mathbb{P}}^2_s(\mathcal{G})\).
\end{proof}
We further need the following corollary of the Hahn-Banach theorem
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:Hahn Banach Corollary}
    Let \(Y\) be a linear subspace of a normed topological vector space \(X\). Let \(x_0\in X\) be such that 
    \begin{equation}
        \inf_{y\in Y}\lVert y-x_0\rVert=d>0.
    \end{equation}
    Then, there exists \(F_0\in X^*\) such that 
    \begin{equation}
        F_0(X_0)=1,\;\sup_{x\in X,\lVert x\rVert\leq 1}|F_0(x)|=\frac{1}{d},\;F_0(y)=0\;\forall y\in Y.
    \end{equation}
\end{lemma}
This is a classic result and essentially follows from the Hahn-Banach theorem (see \parencite[Thm.~5.2]{SteinShakarchi2011FunctionalAnalysis}). For a proof, see \parencite[Thm.~4.8.3.]{Friedman1982FoundationsModernAnalysis}. 
We now have all the technical results in order to prove Lemma \ref{lemma:no approximate profits}.
\begin{proof}[Proof of Lemma \ref{lemma:no approximate profits}]
For the first equivalence, note that for any \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}_s^2(\mathcal{G})\) can be identified with the continuous linear form \(F\in L^2(\mathbb{P})^*\) given by 
\begin{equation}
    F(Z)=\mathbb{E}\Big[\frac{\diff\mathbb{Q}}{\diff\mathbb{P}}Z\Big].
\end{equation}
By definition of \(\mathbb{P}_s^2(\mathcal{G})\) we have that \(F(g)=0\) for all \(g\in\mathcal{G}\) and \(F(1)=1\). In particular,  assume that \(1\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}\). Thus, there exists \((f_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\mathcal{G}\) such that \(\lim_{n\to\infty}F(f_n)=F(1)\). But \(F(f_n)=0,\;\forall n\in\mathbb{N}\), hence the contradiction. Conversly, assume \(1\notin\overline{\mathcal{G}}\). As \(\overline{\mathcal{G}}\) is by definition of the closure a closed subspace of \(L^2\) the condition
\(1\notin\overline{\mathcal{G}}\) implies that 
\begin{equation}
    \inf_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\lVert g-1\rVert_{L^2}>0.
\end{equation}
Notice that this is not garanteed if we merely required \(1\notin\mathcal{G}\).
By Lemma \ref{lemma:Hahn Banach Corollary} there exits a continuous linear form on \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\) such that \(F(g)=0,\;\forall g\in\mathcal{G}\) and  \(F(1)=1\). By the Riesz-Fischer theorem there exists a supportvector \(Z_F\in L^2\) such tha t
\begin{equation}
    F(X)=(Z_F,X)_{L^2}=\mathbb{E}[Z_FX],\;\forall X\in L^2.
\end{equation}
Then the follwoing
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{Q}(A)=\mathbb{E}[Z_F1_A],\;\forall A\in\mathcal{F}.
\end{equation}
defines a signed measure on \((\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\) with \(Z=\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\). In particular, the properties of \(F\) yielded by Lemma \ref{lemma:Hahn Banach Corollary} assure that the signed measure constructed above satisfies \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}_s^2(\mathcal{G})\). This shows the equivalence (1).
\textcolor{blue}{Rest of the proof for (2) and (3)}
\end{proof}

The following observation further suggests that finding the variance-optimal \(\mathcal{G}\)-measure is, in some sense, dual to the problem of approximating risk-less profits in \(\mathcal{G}\):
\bigbreak
For any $g\in\mathcal{G}$ and any $\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_t(\mathcal{G})$, we have
\[
1 \;=\; \mathbb{E}_\mathbb{Q}[1-g]
\;=\; \mathbb{E}\!\bigg[\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}(1-g)\bigg]
\;\le\; \left\|\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}\right\|_{L^2}\,\|1-g\|_{L^2}
\]
by the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality. Therefore
\[
\frac{1}{\inf\limits_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_t(\mathcal{G})}\left\|\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}\right\|_{L^2}}
\;=\;
\sup\limits_{\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_t(\mathcal{G})}\frac{1}{\left\|\frac{d\mathbb{Q}}{d\mathbb{P}}\right\|_{L^2}}
\;\le\;
\inf\limits_{g\in\mathcal{G}}\|1-g\|_{L^2}.
\]
As \(\mathcal{G}\) does not admit approximate profits, the right-hand side is strictly larger than \(0\). If this were not the case, there would exist a sequence in \(\mathcal{G}\) converging to \(1\) in \(L^2\). This implies that \(1\in\overline{\mathcal{G}}^{L^2}\) which contradicts the assumption. Nevertheless, one might ask how close one can get to the risk-less payoff equal to \(1\) by elements in \(\mathcal{G}\). The latter shows that the variance optimal measure yields a lower bound for this question. 
\\\\
The following proposition ties the recently introduced concepts together and shows that, indeed, the mean-variance price can be characterized if \(\mathcal{G}\) does not admit approximate profits, and it is given by the variance optimal signed \(\mathcal{G}\)-measure in the following way.
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:mean variance price}
Suppose that $\mathcal{G}$ is a linear subspace of $L^2$ that admits no
approximate profits in $L^2$. If a contingent claim $H\in L^2$ admits a
$\mathcal{G}$-mean-variance optimal pair $(V_0^*,g^*)$, the
$\mathcal{G}$-approximation price of $H$ is given by
\[
V_0^*=\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathcal{G}}[H],
\]
where $\widetilde{\mathbb{E}}_{\mathcal{G}}$ denotes expectation under the variance-optimal
signed $\mathcal{G}$-martingale measure $\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}$.
\end{proposition}
We provide the proof, as it is rather short and ties together the recently introduced concepts
\begin{proof}
    By Lemma \ref{lemma:no approximate profits} part (2.), if \(\mathcal{G}\) admits no approximate profits, we can orthogonally project \(H\) onto the \(L^2\)-closed subspace \(\overline{\mathcal{A}}=\mathbb{R}+\overline{\mathcal{G}}
    \) to get a unique \(\mathcal{G}\)-mean-variance solution of the form \((V_0^*,g^*)\in \mathbb{R}\times\overline{\mathcal{G}}\). In particular
    \begin{equation}
        H-V_0^*-g^*\in\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{\perp},
    \end{equation}
    where \(\overline{\mathcal{A}}^{\perp}\) is the orthogonal complement of \(\overline{\mathcal{A}}\) in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\). Because \(\widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}\in\mathbb{P}^2_t(\mathcal{G})\) we have 
    \begin{equation}
        V_0^*=\mathbb{E}\bigg[(H-V_0^*-g^*)\frac{\diff \widetilde{\mathbb{P}}_{\mathcal{G}}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\bigg]=0.
    \end{equation}
    Hence, 
    \begin{equation}
