Thus we have shown that \(\alpha\) is measurable with respect to the completion of \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\psi))\) with respect to the measure \(\mathbb{P}_M\). We denote the latter by \(\overline{\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\psi))}^{\mathbb{P}_M}\). By the remarks at the beginning of this proof, this shows that 
     \begin{equation}
        \mathcal{P}\subseteq\overline{\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\psi))}^{\mathbb{P}_M}
    \end{equation}
    But \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\psi))\subseteq\mathcal{P}\) is clear; hence, the claim. 
\end{proof}
In light of our goal of showing the density of \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(cos))\) in \(L^2(M)\), the completion issue in Proposition \ref{prop:E(psi) generated P} seems problematic. Indeed, the monotone class argument Proposition \ref{prop:density of E in L2(M) via monotone class} yields in our present case that the class 
\begin{equation}
     \mathcal{H}=\left\{ \vartheta\in b\mathcal{P}:\vartheta\in L^2(M),\forall \epsilon>0, \exists \vartheta_{\epsilon}\in b\mathcal{E}(cos)\text{ with }\lVert \vartheta_{\epsilon}-\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon\right\}.
\end{equation}
contains all bounded \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(cos))\)-measurable maps. The conclusion then relies on the fact that \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(cos))=\mathcal{P}\).  Hence, Proposition \ref{prop:E(psi) generated P} doesn't allow us to conclude directly. Nevertheless, the completion issue can be dealt with by some standard considerations on null-sets.
\\\\
Recall the well-known fact that for \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\mu}\)-measurable \(\widetilde{f}\)  there exists an \(\mathcal{F}\)-measurable \(f\) such that \(\mu(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:f(\omega)\neq \widetilde{f}(\omega)\right\})=0\).\footnote{Strictly speaking, one should be careful here. The completion of a measure space also involves the extension of a completed measure, which assigns the value \(0\) to every set in \(\mathcal{N}_{\mu}\). We denote it by \(\overline{\mu}\). The set \(\left\{\omega\in\Omega:f(\omega)\neq \widetilde{f}(\omega)\right\}\) is a \(0\)-set with respect to \(\overline{\mu}\) but need not be defined with respect to the original measure, as it needs to be measurable in the raw/uncompleted \(\sigma\)-algebra.} By the monotone class argument, we have for every bounded \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\psi))\)-measurable \(f\), and for every \(\epsilon>0\), there exists \(g_{\epsilon}\in \mathcal{E}(cos)\) such that 
 \begin{equation}
     \lVert f-g_{\epsilon}\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon.
 \end{equation}
 We want to show that \(\mathcal{H}\) contains all bounded \(\overline{\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\cos))}^{\mathbb{P}_M}\)-measurable maps. Let \(\widetilde{f}\) be bounded and \(\overline{\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\cos))}^{\mathbb{P}_M}\)-measurable. There exists a bounded \(\sigma(\mathcal{E}(\cos))\)-measurable \(f\) that is \(\mathbb{P}_M\)-almost everywhere equal to \(\widetilde{f}\)\footnote{By the completion property stated previously; this \(f\) could be unbounded on a \(0\)-measure set with respect to the completed measure. If so, simply take \(f'=-C\wedge f\vee C\), where \(C\) is the bound of \(\widetilde{f}\). Alternatively, just redefine \(f'\) to be \(0\) on a \(\mu\)-null set containing the \(\overline{\mu}\)-null set.} For every \(\epsilon>0\), there is some \(g_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{E}(\cos)\) that approximates \(f\) in \(L^2(M)\). Hence 
 \begin{equation}
     \lVert \widetilde{f}-g_{\epsilon}\rVert_{L^2(M)}=\lVert f-g_{\epsilon}\rVert_{L^2(M)}<\epsilon.
 \end{equation}
 Hence, we can approximate every \(\widetilde{f}\in L^2(\overline{\mathcal{P}}^{\mathbb{P}_M},\mathbb{P}_M)\) hence every \(f\in L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\) as well. We deduce the following result.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:density of E(cos) in L2(M)}
Assume the conditions of Proposition \ref{prop:E(psi) generated P}. Then 
\begin{equation}
    \overline{\mathcal{E}(\cos)}^{L^2(M)}=L^2(M).
\end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{remark}
    Note that we used \(\widetilde{V}^n\to V\) \(\mathbb{P}_M\) almost surely. We could then consider the completion of the measure space \((\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+, \mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\) with respect to \(\mathbb{P}_M\). This allowed us to argue about approximation in \(L^2(M)=L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\) and the completed space \(L^2(M)=L^2(\mathcal{P},\mathbb{P}_M)\). More precisely, we use the fact that 
    \begin{equation}
        L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})\simeq  L^2(\Omega,\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{P}},\mathbb{P}),
    \end{equation}
    as quotient spaces, that is, under the identification \(X\sim Y\) if \(\lVert X-Y\rVert_{L^2}=0\),\footnote{ Again, strictly speaking, one has to consider the space \(L^2(\Omega,\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{P}},\overline{\mathbb{P}})\), where \(\overline{\mathbb{P}}\) is the unique extension of \(\mathbb{P}\) to \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{P}}\). Then the correct statement is that \(L^2(\Omega,\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{P}},\overline{\mathbb{P}})=L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\overline{\mathbb{P}})\),, where \(\mathcal{F}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{P}}\) is a sub-\(\sigma\)-algebra. Hence, \(L^2(\Omega,\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{P}},\overline{\mathbb{P}})\subseteq L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\overline{\mathbb{P}})\) is clear, and the converse containment follows from the identification of \(f\in L^2(\Omega,\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{P}},\overline{\mathbb{P}})\) with its \(\mathcal{F}\)-measurable version \(\widetilde{f}\) such that \(\lVert f-\overline{f}\rVert_{L^2(\overline{\mathbb{P}})}\); hence, \(f=\widetilde{f}\) in the \(L^2(\overline{\mathbb{P}})\)-sense. In particular, we abuse notation slightly in the main text by identifying \(\mathbb{P}\) and \(\overline{\mathbb{P}}\). In particular, they only differ on sets \(A\subseteq N\) where \(\mathbb{P}(N)=0\) and \(A\in\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{\mathbb{P}}/\mathcal{F}\).}
    The argument thus crucially uses that for \(p=2\), \(L^p(M)=L^2(\mathbb{P}_M)\). Thus this argument yields the claim for \(p=2\). Fortunately, this is the interesting case for the mean-variance hedging results. 
\end{remark}
The remaining task is to show that
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{E}(\cos)\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{E}(\psi)}^{L^2(M)}
\end{equation}
for any activation function \(\psi\) and \(p\in[1,\infty)\). Indeed, if this holds, together with Lemma \ref{lemma:density of E(cos) in L2(M)} we get 
\begin{equation}
    \overline{\mathcal{E}(\psi)}^{L^2(M)}=L^2(M).
\end{equation}
for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2\), without further infinitesimal integrability conditions. 
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:density of E(psi) in L^2(M)}
    Assume \(Y_t\) takes values in a compact subspace \(K^t\in\mathbb{R}\) for every \(t\in\mathbb{R}_+\). Then, under the assumptions of Proposition \ref{prop:E(psi) generated P}, we have for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2\)
    \begin{equation}
        \overline{\mathcal{E}(\psi)}^{L^2(M)}=L^2(M)\,.
    \end{equation}
    for any \(\psi\) satisfying the standing assumption Definition \ref{def:standing assumptions}.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
As explained it remainse to show that 
    \begin{equation}
    \mathcal{E}(\cos)\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{E}(\psi)}^{L^2(M)}
\end{equation}
Let \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\cos)\):
\begin{equation}
    \vartheta = \vartheta_01_{\left\{0\right\}}+\sum_{i=1}^n \varphi_i(Y_{t_1}^i,\dots,Y_{t_{k(i)}}^i)1_{(t_i,t_{i+1}]}
\end{equation}
with \(\varphi_i\in\mathcal{NN}_{\cos}(\mathbb{R}^{k(i)})\). Note that for every \(i=0,\dots,n\), \((Y_{t_1}^i,\dots,Y_{t_{k(i)}}^i)\) takes values in \(K^{t_1}\times\dots\times K^{t_{k(i)}}\) which is a compact subset of \(\mathbb{R}^{k(i)}\). Hence 
\begin{equation}
    \varphi_i=\sum_{j=0}^m\alpha_{ij}\cos(A_{ij}),
\end{equation}
where \(A_{ji}:\mathbb{R}^{k(i)}\to\mathbb{R}\) is affine linear. 
By \parencite[Thm.~2]{Hornik1991}, for the continuous activation function \(\cos\) and with the compact set \(K^i=K^{t_1}\times\dots\times K^{t_{k(i)}}\), for every \(\epsilon>0\) there exists \(\vartheta_{ij}^{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}(K)\) such that 
\begin{equation}
    \sup_{x\in K^i}|\cos(A_{ij}(x))-\vartheta_{ij}^{\epsilon}(x)|<\epsilon
\end{equation}
where \(\vartheta_{ij}^{\epsilon}=\sum_{k=1}^l\beta_{ijk}\psi(B_{ijk})\) for some affine linear \(B_{ijk}\). Setting
\begin{equation}
    \xi = \widetilde{\varphi}_i(Y_{t_1},\dots,Y_{t_{k(0)}})1_{\left\{ 0\right\}}+\sum_{i=1}^n\widetilde{\varphi}_i(Y_{t_1},\dots,Y_{t_{k(i)}})1_{(t_i,t_{i+1}]}
\end{equation}
with \(\widetilde{\varphi}_i(Y_{t_1},\dots,Y_{t_{k(i)}})=\sum_{j=0}^m\alpha_{ij}\vartheta_{ij}^{\epsilon(ij)}\). We choose \(\epsilon(ij)=\frac{\epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}}{(n+1)(m+1)|\alpha_{ij}|}\)
we get 
\begin{align*}
    \sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+]}|\vartheta_t-\xi_t|\leq \sum_{i=0}^{n}|\widetilde{\varphi}_i(Y_{t_1},\dots,Y_{t_{k(i)}})-\varphi_i(Y_{t_1},\dots,Y_{t_{k(i)}})|\leq \sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j=0}^m|\alpha_{ij}||\cos(A_{ij}(x))-\vartheta_{ij}^{\epsilon}(x)|
\end{align*}
with
\begin{equation}
    \sup_{x\in K^i}(\vartheta-\xi)_{\infty}^*\leq \sum_{i=0}^{n}\sum_{j=0}^m|\alpha_{ij}|\sup_{x\in K^i}|\cos(A_{ij}(x))-\vartheta_{ij}^{\epsilon}(x)|< \epsilon^{\frac{1}{2}}
\end{equation}
Finally we can bound the \(L^2(M)\) norm as follows
\begin{align*}
    \mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}(\xi_t-\vartheta_t)^2\diff [M]_t\right)\bigg]\leq \mathbb{E}\Big[((\xi-\vartheta)_{\infty}^*)[M]_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big]\leq\epsilon\mathbb{E}\Big[[M]_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}\Big].
\end{align*}
By assumption \(\mathbb{E}[[M]_{\infty}^{\frac{1}{2}}]\lesssim\lVert M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^2}^2<\infty\), the claim follows. 
\end{proof}
The compactness assumption on the image of \(Y_t\) is unpleasant and is violated in basic examples. Indeed, assume that our market information is given by the price process, which is taken to be a Brownian motion. Then, by the law of iterated logarithm \parencite[Thm.~2.1]{Schweizer2025BrownianMotionStochasticCalculus}, it has almost surely unbounded paths. To get rid of the compactness assumption, we observe the following. We are only considering \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) at the moment. The relevant approximation property from the second part of Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies} does not require \(Y\) to be Lévy. So we might as well consider 
\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{Y}_t=\tanh(Y_t)\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
\end{equation} 
Then clearly 
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{F}=\sigma(Y_t:t\in\mathbb{R}_+)=\sigma(\widetilde{Y}_t:t\in\mathbb{R}_+)=\widetilde{\mathcal{F}},
\end{equation}
because \(\tanh:\mathbb{R}\to(-1,1)\) is injective, hence \(Y_t=\tanh^{-1}\circ\tanh\circ Y_t\). The same clearly holds for the respective \(\mathbb{P}\)-completion. Denote by \(\widetilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\) the class of random variables analogous to \(\Theta_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\) with \(\widetilde{Y}\) instead of \(Y\); that is, \(Z\in\widetilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\) has the representation.
\begin{equation}
    Z =f(\widetilde{Y}_{t_1},\dots, \widetilde{Y}_{t_n}).
\end{equation}
for some \(f\in\mathcal{NN}_{\psi}((\mathbb{R}^{dn})^*)\). 
Denote by \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\) the set of processes of the form
\begin{equation}
  \widetilde{\vartheta}_t=\widetilde{\varphi}_01_{\left\{0\right\}}(t)+\sum_{i=1}^n\widetilde{\varphi}_i1_{(t_I,t_{i+1}]}(t),
  \end{equation}
 where \(\widetilde{\varphi}_i\in\widetilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\).  
 \\\\
 Observe that we can simply replace \({\Theta}_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\) by \(\widetilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\) in the second part of  Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies} to get that \(\widetilde{\Theta}_{\mathcal{NN}}(t,\psi)\) is dense in \(L^p(\overline{{\mathcal{F}_{t_i}}})\). Again, this is because the Lévy-assumption of \(Y\) is only necessary for the stopped versions, that is the third part of Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies}, where one needs \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}_{\tau}=\sigma(\tau)\vee\sigma(Y_t^{\tau}:t\in J)\vee\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\). However, in the deterministic case of \(\mathcal{E}\) and \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\), we don't need \(Y\) to be Lévy. For the relevant first part of Proposition \ref{prop:approximation of measurable maps by algorithmic strategies} the arguments are unchanged for \(\widetilde{Y}\) instead of \(Y\). Indeed, the crucial Doob-Dynkin application for the process 
 \begin{equation}
     \widetilde{M}_n=\mathbb{E}[Z_{\mathcal{F}_I}|\widetilde{\mathcal{F}}_n]
 \end{equation}
 yields the existence of a measurable map \(\widetilde{f}_n\) such that
 \begin{equation}
      \widetilde{M}_n=\widetilde{f}_n(\widetilde{Y}_{j_1},\dots,\widetilde{Y}_{j_n})\,.
 \end{equation}
 The argument ist those completely analogous.
 
 
 From a conceptual point of view, the trader/seller observes the market information through \(\mathcal{F}\) and the filtration \((\mathcal{F}_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\) generated by the market information process to be \(Y\) or \(\widetilde{Y}\); it should be regarded as a change of coordinates, as long as he only asks questions on deterministic times, not random times. 
 \\\\
 In particular, we have density of \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\) in \(L^2(M)\). Crucially, considering \(\widetilde{Y}\) instead of \(Y\) has the following advantage:
\begin{equation}
\widetilde{Y}_t=\tanh(Y_t)\in(-1,1)\subseteq[-1,1]\quad\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+,
\end{equation}
hence \(\widetilde{Y}\) takes values in a compact subset of \(\mathbb{R}\). Instead of requiring that the image of \(Y_t\) is included in a compact subset for every \(t\) we might consider \(\widetilde{Y}\) instead. 
\bigbreak
We get the following version of Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)}
\begin{proposition}\label{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi) under compacteness}
    Let \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^{2}\). Then the set \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\) is dense in \(L^2(M)\) with respect to the seminorm on \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M)}\). Moreover, for every \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\), there exists a sequence \((\widetilde{\vartheta^n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\), such that
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\int \widetilde{\vartheta^n}\diff M=\int \vartheta\diff M,
    \end{equation}
    where convergence holds in \(\mathcal{H}^2\). 
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    The density of \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\) in \(L^2(M)\) is analogous to Proposition \ref{prop:density of E(psi) in L^2(M)}. The last claim follows the exact same reasoning as in Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)}.
\end{proof}
This version does not require the additional infinitesimal integrability of \(M\).  


\chapter{Deep mean--variance hedging in continuous time}\label{ch:deep mean-square hedging in continuous time}


\section{Deep mean--variance hedging in the martingale case}
We finally state one of the main results we were building up to: deep mean-variance hedging. It is formulated in the square-integrable setup and thus should be seen as complementary to the mean-variance discussion of Section \ref{sec:Mean Variance Hedging in square-integrable setting}. In particular, the claim is that instead of looking among all the terminal values of portfolios in \((V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times L^2(M)\) in order to approximate the liability, we can restrict our search to \(\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{E}(\psi)\). The error made by looking only in that subset is arbitrarily small. The exact mathematical statement is as follows. 
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}
Assume there exists \(\epsilon>0\) such that \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^{2+\epsilon}\) and let \(H\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) be a real-valued \(\mathcal{F}_{\infty}\)-measurable random variable. Furthermore, assume \(\mathcal{F}_0\) to be trivial.  Then
\begin{equation}\label{eq:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}
    \min_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in L^2(M)}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(V_0+\int_{0}^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff M_t-H\bigg)^2\bigg]
    \,=\,\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(V_0+\int_{0}^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff M_t-H\bigg)^2\bigg],
\end{equation}
where the minimum on the left-hand side is unique. 
In particular, there are two statements: 1) The existence and uniqueness of the minimum for the left-hand side of \eqref{eq:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}
\\2) The equality of both expressions. 
\end{theorem}

\begin{proof}[Proof of Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}]
    The existence of the left-hand side follows from the results in Section \ref{sec:Mean Variance Hedging in square-integrable setting} and is a consequence of the Hilbert projection theorem. Denote by \((V_0^*,\vartheta^*)\in\mathbb{R}\times L^2(M)\) the unique strategy that achieves the minimum in \eqref{eq:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}. It remains to show 2). Observe that is it enought to show that for any \(\delta>0\) there exists a strategy \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) such that 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:delta property}
       \lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta^*\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}+\delta\geq \lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}.
    \end{equation}
Indeed, because \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\subseteq L^2(M)\), we have 
\begin{equation*}
    \min_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in L^2(M)}\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}\leq \inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)}\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}.
\end{equation*}
Conversly, if \eqref{eq:delta property} holds, we have
\begin{align*}
     \min_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in L^2(M)}\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}+\delta&=\lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta^*\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}+\delta
     \\&\geq \lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}
     \\&\geq\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R}}\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}
     \\&\geq \inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)}\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}.
\end{align*}
This shows that 
\begin{equation*}
    \min_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in L^2(M)}\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}\geq\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)}\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}
\end{equation*}
and hence equality.


    By Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)}, for every \(\delta>0\) there exists \(\vartheta\in\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) such that \(\lVert \vartheta
    ^*\bullet M-\vartheta\bullet M\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^2}\leq\delta\). Hence
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}&\leq\lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta^*\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}+\lVert (\vartheta^*\bullet M)_{\infty}-(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2}\\
        &\leq\lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta^*\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}+\lVert (\vartheta^*\bullet M)-(\vartheta\bullet M)\rVert_{\mathcal{H}^2}
        \\&\leq\lVert V_0^*+(\vartheta^*\bullet M)_{\infty}-H\rVert_{L^2}+\delta.
    \end{align*}
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}
    As Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging} is formulated for \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\), according to the discussion leading up to Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi) under compacteness}, we might as well substitute \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) with \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\) and let go of the extra \(\epsilon\)-integrability.
\end{remark}

\begin{remark}\label{rem:sufficient condition for extension}
      Notice that what made the proof of the second part work was essentially the continuity of the map \(\vartheta\mapsto(\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}\) as a continuous linear operator from \(L^2(M)\) to \(L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\mathbb{P})\). This is the essential idea that is going to lead us to extend Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging} to the semimartingale setting. 
\end{remark}
 


\section{Extension for semimartingales}\label{sec:extension of deep mean squared hedging to semimartingales}
Regarding Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}, one is led to the natural question of whether one can obtain the claim for a more general setup, allowing for more general integrators. In this section, we will investigate one particular approach. More precisely, we will allow a subclass of semimartingales as price processes \(X\). For this, we will have to adapt the space of integrable processes in a way that density results like \cref{cor:p-ucp density of,cor:ucp and p-ucp density of E} still apply. We will first outline the general idea before giving concrete conditions and the corresponding extension. This section is structured in a didactic way, so that all definitions are motivated and intuitive. For what is to follow, we consider all stochastic processes to be real-valued processes, in particular one-dimensional. 



Let \(X\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm{\rm{loc}}}^2\) be a locally square-integrable special semimartingale. with unique canonical decomposition
\begin{equation}\label{eq:canonical decomposition}
    X=X_0+M+A,
\end{equation}
where \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{\rm{loc}}}\) is a locally square-integrable local martingale starting at \(0\) and \(A\), of finite variation, predictable, starting at \(0\) and with locally square-integrable total variation. For a special martingale.



The existence of a solution to the mean variance hedging problem for semimartingales is a separate issue. We treated it partly in Section \ref{sec:Mean-variance Hedging with continuous price process} and mainly refer to \parencite{Schweizer2001GuidedTourQuadraticHedging} for a good overview and \parencite{Schweizer1994ApproximatingRandomVariablesStochasticIntegrals} for the exhaustive treatment. By \cref{rem:sufficient condition for extension} we are interested in bounding the \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\)-norm of \((\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty}\). The following bounds motivate the further definitions. Let \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\):
\begin{align}\label{eq:bounding-L2-norm-stochastic-integral}
    \lVert (\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}
    &\leq \lVert (\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}+\lVert (\vartheta\bullet A)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}\\
    &\leq\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)} +\lVert (|\vartheta|\bullet |A|)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})},
\end{align}
where we used the triangle inequality in the first step and the Itô isometry, as well as the bound
\begin{equation}
    \bigg|\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff A_t\bigg|\leq \int_0^{\infty}|\vartheta_t|\diff |A|_t,
\end{equation} 
for the second inequality. We want to read the latter as a continuity statement for the linear operator \(I_{\infty}:\vartheta\to (\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty}\) between normed spaces \( (\mathcal{D},\left\lvert\!\left\lvert\!\left\lvert \cdot\right\rvert\!\right\rvert\!\right\rvert)\to(L^2(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F}_{\infty}),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2})\), that is,
\begin{equation}
    \lVert (\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}\lesssim \left\lvert\!\left\lvert\!\left\lvert \vartheta\right\rvert\!\right\rvert\!\right\rvert
\end{equation}
for some norm \(\left\lvert\!\left\lvert\!\left\lvert \cdot\right\rvert\!\right\rvert\!\right\rvert\). By the above, the natural candidate for \(\mathcal{D}\) is the following space
\begin{definition}\label{def:L2MA}
Let \(X\) be a special semimartingale, as in \eqref{eq:canonical decomposition}. Then we define the following
\begin{align*}
     \mathcal{L}^2(M,A)\coloneqq\left\{\vartheta \text{ predictable}:\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}+\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_0^{\infty} |\vartheta|_t\diff |A|_t\right)^2\bigg]^{\frac{1}{2}}\eqqcolon\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}<\infty\right\},
\end{align*}
    Then, as usual, we define 
    \begin{equation}
        L^2(M,A)=\mathcal{L}^2(M,A)/\sim,
    \end{equation}
    where \(X\sim Y\) if \(\lVert X-Y\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}=0\). 
    Further, we introduce the following set
 \begin{equation}
        \mathcal{L}^2(A)=\left\{\vartheta \text{ predictable }:\lVert\int_{0}^{\infty}|\vartheta_t|\diff |A|_t\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}\eqqcolon\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}<\infty\right\}
   \end{equation}
    and the corresponding quotient space \(L^2(A)\).
\end{definition}
With this notation we have
\begin{equation}
    L^2(M,A)=L^2(A)\cap L^2(M)
\end{equation}
and \begin{equation}\label{eq:norm-L2MA}
    \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}=\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}+\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}.
\end{equation}
As usual, we will abuse notation in talking about elements in \(L^2(M,A)\) as processes, although we implicitly pick representatives of the corresponding equivalence class.
 Furthermore, we have remarked in Definition \ref{def:X integrable processes for X in S^2_loc} that processes in \(L(M,A)\) are \(X\)-integrable for \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{\rm{loc}}}\) and  \(\int \vartheta\diff X\) is in \(\mathcal{S}^2\). Also, it is clear by construction that 
\(\Theta=L^2(M,A)\subseteq \Theta^{(2)}\). 
Recall that \(L^2(M,A)=\Theta_S\) in the notation of Section \ref{sec:Mean-variance Hedging with continuous price process}. Furthermore, it is clear that \(L^2(M,A)\) is the natural extension to the \(L^2(M)\) case discussed in Section \ref{sec:Mean Variance Hedging in square-integrable setting}. Finally, we observe that \(\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}=0\) if and only if \(\vartheta\bullet X\) is almost surely the \(0\) process and that \((L^2(M,A),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M,A)})\) is a Banach space. We show the completeness of the latter in the following proposition
\begin{proposition}
    Let \(L^2(M,A)=L^2(M)\cap L^2(A)\) be defined as in Definition \ref{def:L2MA} and equipped with the norm \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}\) as defined in \eqref{eq:norm-L2MA}. Then \((L^2(M,A),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M,A)})\) is a complete metric space, that is, every Cauchy sequence converges.
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    The following proof follows the same proof strategy used to show the completeness of \(L^p(\mu)\) space \parencite[Thm.~4.5]{LeGall2022MeasureTheoryProbabilityStochasticProcesses}. We consider a fast-decreasing Cauchy sequence and use it to construct an almost sure limit process, which we take as a  candidate for our limit process in \(L^2(M,A)\). Ont then needs to show that the pointwise limit is also a \(L^2(M,A)\)-limit to conclude the proof. The main difficulty in the case \(L^2(M,A)\) compared to the classic \(L^p(\mu)\)-case arises from the fact that \(L^2(A)\) is not a pure \(L^2(\mu)\) space for some measure \(\mu\) on the space of predictable processes but a mix of \(L^2\) and \(L^1\). 
    \bigbreak
    Let \((\vartheta_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq L^2(M,A)\) be a Cauchy sequence in \((L^2(M,A),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M,A)})\). 
    Without loss of generality, assume 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:fastly converging cauchy}
        \lVert \vartheta^{n+1}-\vartheta^{n}\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}\leq\frac{1}{2^n}.
    \end{equation}
    If this is not the case, consider a subsequence \((\vartheta_{n(k)})_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\) that satisfies the above. If we show that a subsequence of a Cauchy sequence converges in norm, we conclude that the original Cauchy sequence converges to that same limit.

    
    Furthermore, observe that the definition of \eqref{eq:norm-L2MA} implies that \((\vartheta^n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) is a Cauchy sequence in \(L^2(M)\) and \(L^2(A)\) separately, and \eqref{eq:fastly converging cauchy} holds in both spaces separately as well. 
    Consider the formal expression
    \begin{equation}
        g=|\vartheta_1|+\sum_{n=1}^\infty|\vartheta^{n+1}-\vartheta^{n}|,
    \end{equation}
    where the series may or may not be finite at this point. For the following, we denote the partial sums as follows
    \begin{equation}
        g_N=|\vartheta_1|+\sum_{n=1}^N|\vartheta^{n+1}-\vartheta^{n}|.
    \end{equation}
    We show that \(g\in L^2(M,A)\). We do this by arguing that \(g\in L^2(M)\) and \(g\in L^2(A)\). Clearly, \(g\) is predictable as a pointwise limit of predictable processes \(g_N\), which are in turn predictable as a finite sum of predictable processes \(\vartheta^i\).
    
    
    Recall that \(L^2(M)\simeq L^2(\mathbb{P}_M)\) for the measure on the predictable \(\sigma\)-algebra
    \begin{equation}
    \mathbb{P}_M:B\mapsto\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}1_B^2\diff\langle M\rangle\bigg],\;B\in\mathcal{P}.
    \end{equation}
    Hence, by monotone convergence and the Minkowski inequality (\parencite[Thm.~4.4]{LeGall2022MeasureTheoryProbabilityStochasticProcesses})
    \begin{equation}
        \lVert g\rVert_{L^2(\mu_M)}=\lVert \lim_{N\to\infty}g_N\rVert_{L^2(\mu_M)}=\lim_{N\to\infty}\lVert g_N\rVert_{L^2(\mu_M)}\leq \sum_{n=1}^\infty\lVert \vartheta^{n+1}-\vartheta^n\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P}_M)}<\infty,
    \end{equation}
    where we used in the last step that the series is bounded from above by the converging series \(\sum_{n=1}^\infty\frac{1}{2^n}<\infty\). For the \(L^2(A)\) part, we need to argue by successive application of monotone convergence.
    \begin{align*}
    \mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_0^{\infty}\lim_{N\to\infty}g_N\diff|A|\right)^2\bigg]&=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\lim_{N\to\infty}\left(\int_0^{\infty} g_N\diff|A|\right)^2\bigg]
    \\&=\lim_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_0^{\infty} g_N\diff|A|\right)^2\bigg]\leq \sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\lVert \vartheta^{n+1}-\vartheta^{n}\rVert_{L^2(A)}<\infty,
    \end{align*}
    where the first equality is due to monotone convergence for every \(\omega\) of the increasing sequence \(g_N(\omega)\), the second equality is again due to monotone convergence for the increasing sequence \(\int_0^{\infty}g_N\diff |A|\), and the last inequality follows from the Minkowski inequality for \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\). This shows that
    \begin{equation}
        g\in L^2(M,A).
    \end{equation}
    Next, we want to argue that \(g<\infty\) pointwise on a large set. We have, in particular, that \(g\in L^2(M)\). Hence, by \parencite[Prop.~2.7]{LeGall2022MeasureTheoryProbabilityStochasticProcesses} we have 
    \begin{equation}
        g<\infty,\;\mathbb{P}_M\text{-a.s.}
    \end{equation}
    Concretely, this means that there exists a predictable set \(\mathcal{N}\) such that \(\mathbb{P}(\mathcal{N})=0\) and 
    \begin{equation}
        g(\omega,t)=\sum_{n=1}^\infty|\vartheta^{n+1}(\omega,t)-\vartheta^n(\omega,t)|<\infty,\;(\omega,t)\in \mathcal{N}^{c}.
    \end{equation}
    For the \(L^2(A)\) part, the matter is more delicate. 
    We want to show that for
    \begin{equation}
        \mathcal{D}=\left\{(\omega,t):g(\omega,t)=\infty\right\}
    \end{equation}
     we have 
     \begin{equation}
         \mathbb{P}_{|A|}(\mathcal{D}) = \mathbb{E}[\int_0^{\infty}1_\mathcal{D}\diff |A|]=0
     \end{equation}
     where \(\diff A:B\mapsto \mathbb{E}[\int_0^{\infty}1_B\diff |A|]\) defines a measure on \((\Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+,\mathcal{P})\) contrary to \(B\mapsto \mathbb{E}[\left(\int_0^{\infty}1_B\diff |A|\right)^2]\). First, notice that \(\mathcal{D}\) is a predictable set as it can be written as a countable intersection of predictable sets
     \begin{equation}
         \mathcal{D}=\bigcap_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\left\{g\geq n\right\}.
     \end{equation}
Writing \(\mathcal{D}_{\omega}=\left\{t\in\mathbb{R}_+:g(t,\omega)=\infty\right\}\) for fixed \(\omega\in\Omega\) we have 
     \begin{align}
         \mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}1_\mathcal{D}\diff |A|\bigg]=\int_{\Omega}\left(\int_0^{\infty}1_{\mathcal{D}_{\omega}}(t)\diff |A|_t(\omega)\right)\diff \mathbb{P}(\omega)
     \end{align}
     but by
     \begin{equation}
          \mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}g\diff |A|\right)^2\bigg]<\infty\implies \int_{0}^{\infty}g(\omega,t)\diff |A|_t(\omega)<\infty
     \end{equation}
     for almost all \(\omega\in \Omega\).
  In other words, \(\mathbb{P}\)--almost surely, \(\mathcal{N}_{\omega}\) is a \(\mathbb{P}_{|A|}(\omega)\)--null set, hence in particular \(\mathbb{E}[\int_0^\infty 1_\mathcal{N}\diff|A|]=\int_{\Omega}\big(\int_0^\infty 1_{\mathcal{N}_{\omega}}\diff |A|(\omega)\big)\diff\mathbb{P}(\omega)=0\). That means
     \begin{equation}
         g<\infty,\; \diff |A|\text{-a.s.}
     \end{equation}
 Hence, there exists a \(\mathbb{P}_M\)-null set \(\mathcal{N}\) and a \(\diff |A|\)-null set \(\mathcal{D}\) such that \(g<\infty\) on these sets respectively. 
 Consider the following telescopic sum
 \begin{equation}
     h(\omega,t) = \vartheta^1(\omega,t)+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\left(\vartheta^{n+1}(\omega,t)-\vartheta^n(\omega,t)\right)=\lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta^n(\omega,t),\;(\omega,t)\in \mathcal{N}^c\cup \mathcal{D}^c
 \end{equation}
and set \(h=0\) on \(\mathcal{N}\cap \mathcal{D}\). As the predictable set \(\mathcal{N}\cap \mathcal{D}\) is both a \(\mathbb{P}_M\)-null set and a \(\mathbb{P}_{|A|}\)-null set we have that 
\begin{equation}
    \lim_{n\to\infty}\vartheta^n=h
\end{equation}
pointwise, both \(\diff |A|\)-almost surely and \(\mathbb{P}_M\)-almost surely.
Observe that \(\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}|\vartheta^{n+1}-\vartheta^n|<\infty\) for \((\omega,t)\in \mathcal{N}^c\cup \mathcal{D}^c\). Furthermore, by \parencite[Lemma.~1.15]{LeGall2022MeasureTheoryProbabilityStochasticProcesses} \(h\) is measurable. We want to show that \(h\in L^2(M,A)\). Again, we show that it is in \(L^2(M)\), and that it is in \(L^2(A)\) as well. But both clearly follow from 
\begin{equation}
    |h|\leq g
\end{equation}
and the monotonicity of the norms on \(L^2(M)\) and \(L^2(A)\), which in turn follow from the monotonicity of \(x\mapsto x^p\) and the Lebesgue integral. 


It remains to show that 
\begin{equation}
    \vartheta^n\overset{L^2(M,A)}{\to}h.
\end{equation}
Equivalently, we show that \(\lVert\vartheta^n-h\rVert_{L^2(M)}\to 0\) and \(\lVert\vartheta^n-h\rVert_{L^2(A)}\to 0\). Observe that
\begin{equation}
    |h-\vartheta^n|^2\leq 4g^2, \forall n\in\mathbb{N}
\end{equation}
everywhere. Indeed, on \(\mathcal{N}^c\cup \mathcal{D}^c\) this holds by the triangle inequality and the fact that
\begin{align}
    |\vartheta^n|=|\vartheta^1+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}\vartheta^{n+1}-\vartheta^n]|\leq |\vartheta^1|+\sum_{k=1}^{n-1}|\vartheta^{n+1}-\vartheta^n|]\leq g
\end{align}
and on \(\mathcal{N}\cap \mathcal{D}\) we defined \(h=0\), hence the same bound holds.
and we have already shown that \(g\in L^2(M)\) and \(L^2(A)\). Hence, by dominated convergence, we get 
\begin{equation}
    \lVert h-\vartheta^n\rVert_{L^2(M)}\to 0.
\end{equation}
Similarly, as \(\int_0^{\infty}g(\omega,t)\diff |A|_t(\omega)<\infty\) \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost surely, we have for almost all \(\omega\), by dominated convergence
\begin{equation}
    \int_0^{\infty}|h(\omega,t)-\vartheta^n(\omega,t)|\diff|A|_t(\omega)\to 0.
\end{equation}
Then, due to \(\int_0^{\infty}|h(\omega,t)-\vartheta^n(\omega,t)|\diff|A|_t(\omega)\lesssim\int_0^{\infty}|g(\omega,t)|\diff|A|_t(\omega)\), again by dominated convergence
\begin{equation}
     \lVert h-\vartheta^n\rVert_{L^2(A)}\to 0.
\end{equation}
which concludes the proof.
\end{proof}
\bigbreak
 By construction, or more precisely by \eqref{eq:bounding-L2-norm-stochastic-integral} we have for processes \(\vartheta\in L^2(A,M)\) that the map
 \begin{align*}\label{eq:final integral map}
    I_{\infty}:\;&\vartheta\mapsto (\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty},
     \\&L^2(M,A)\to L^2(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F}_{\infty})
 \end{align*} is continuous.

 Hence, an approximating sequence of \(\vartheta\in L(M,A)\) yields an approximating sequence of the integral \((\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty}\) in \(L^2\). We have seen in Proposition \ref{prop:density of E in Lp(M) via monotone class} that we can approximate \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\) by elements in \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\). We thus need to argue under what assumptions we can approximate \(\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)\) by elements of \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\). Our approach is based on the observations that both \(L^2(M)\) and  \(L^2(M,A)\) are defined through their norm on the space of predictable processes. Hence, if we find conditions on the semimartingale \(X\) such that both norms are equivalent, then the spaces \(L^2(M)\) and \(L^2(M,A)\) contain the same processes and thus, \(L^2(M)=L^2(M,A)\) as normed spaces. In particular, if \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) is dense in \(L^2(M)\), it is clearly dense in \(L^2(M,A)\) as well. Thus, we get a deep mean variance hedging result for an appropriate class of semimartingales \(X\). 

 
This motivates further investigation of conditions for which the norms \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M)}\) and \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}\) are equivalent. First, notice that for all integrable processes \(\vartheta\) we clearly have
\begin{equation}
    \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}\leq\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}.
\end{equation}
Hence, we want to understand under which conditions there exists \(C>0\) such that 
\begin{equation}
    \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}\leq C \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)},\quad \forall \theta \in L^2(M)\cap L^2(A)\,.
\end{equation}
or equivalently 
\begin{equation}\label{condition_D_2(P)}
    \exists C>0:\quad \lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}\leq C\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)},\quad \forall\vartheta\in L^2(A)\cap L^2(M).
\end{equation}
For notational consistency with \parencite{DelbaenMonatSchachermayerSchweizerStricker1997WeightedNormInequalities} we write 
\begin{equation}
    X\in D_2(\mathbb{P})
\end{equation}
if the semimartingale \(X\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm{loc}}^2\) satisfies \cref{condition_D_2(P)}. Notice that \cref{condition_D_2(P)} implies 
\begin{equation}
    L^2(M)\subseteq L^2(A).
\end{equation}
Hence, if \cref{condition_D_2(P)} holds for \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\cap L^2(A)\), it thus hold for \(\vartheta\in L^2(M)\) as well and we have 
\begin{equation}
    D_2(\mathbb{P})\implies L^2(M,A)=L^2(M).
\end{equation} 
The statement is actually an if and only if statement. See \parencite[Lemma.~3.2.]{DelbaenMonatSchachermayerSchweizerStricker1997WeightedNormInequalities}.
\bigbreak
First, we give sufficient conditions for \cref{condition_D_2(P)} by using simple bounds. After this we give necessary conditions. In order to relate the \(L^2(A)\) norm with the \(L^2(M)\)-norm, a natural condition is to assume the following.
\begin{equation}\label{eq:absolute continuity}
    \diff A_t\ll \diff\langle M\rangle_t,\;\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.},\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+,
\end{equation}
with predictable density \(\lambda\).
That is 
\begin{equation}
    \exists \lambda \text{ predictable}:\; \diff A_t=\lambda_t \diff \langle M\rangle_t\quad\;\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.},\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
\end{equation}
Observe two things. First \eqref{eq:absolute continuity} is to be understood as the absolute continuity of the pathwise Lebesgue-Stieltjes measures. Defining the Doléans measure for a finite variation process \(A\) on the predictable \(\sigma\)-algebra as
\begin{equation}\label{eq:lebesgue-stieltjes for processes} \mathbb{P}_A(B)=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}1_B(\omega,t)\diff A_t(\omega)\bigg]
\end{equation}
for all predictable sets \(B\), we might rewrite \eqref{eq:absolute continuity} as 
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{P}_A=\lambda\mathbb{P}_{\langle M\rangle}.
\end{equation}
Notice, that for \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{loc}\), \(\mathbb{P}_{\langle M\rangle}=\mathbb{P}_{[M]}\eqqcolon\mathbb{P}_{M}\) is the measure we have encountered in Definition \ref{def:P_M measure}.

From the well-known fact that for some finite variation function \(f\) the Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure of its variation is the total variation measure of the Lebesgue-Stieltjes measure of \(f\), we have in particular (see \parencite[rem.~2.6]{DelbaenSchachermayer1994FTAP})
\begin{equation}
    \diff |A|=|\lambda|\diff \langle M\rangle.
\end{equation}
Lastly, note that we assume absolute continuity with respect to the process \(\langle M\rangle\), and not \([M]\). Indeed, as remarked in Definition \ref{def:L^p(M)}, this does not matter with regard to the definition of the \(L^2(M)\)-norm. However, by \parencite[Thm.~7]{AnselStricker1992LoisMartingale} it is only for the predictable process \(\langle M\rangle\) that we know the density \(\lambda\) can be taken to be predictable.
If \eqref{eq:absolute continuity} holds, we have, in particular, (using \(\langle M\rangle\) instead of \([M]\))

\begin{align}\label{eq:bounding-L2MA-norm-necessary-assumption}
\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}^2
 =&\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg]
 +\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_0^{\infty} |\vartheta_t||\lambda_t|\diff\langle M\rangle_t\bigg)^2\bigg]\\
&\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg]
 +\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\cdot\int_0^{\infty}\lambda_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg]\notag \\
&\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\int_0^{\infty} \vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg]
 +\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg)^{p}\bigg]^{1/p}\cdot\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(\int_0^{\infty}\lambda_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\bigg)^{q}\bigg]^{1/q}\notag 
,
\end{align}
where we used the pathwise Cauchy--Schwarz inequality in the second step and Hölder's inequality in the third step. In the case \(p=1,q=\infty\) the above reads
\begin{equation}
    \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}^2\leq\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}^2\left(1+\lVert\int_0^{\infty}\lambda_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t\rVert_{\infty}\right).
\end{equation}
We define the mean-variance tradeoff (MVT)  
\begin{equation}
    K_t\coloneqq\int_0^{t}(\lambda_s)^2\diff \langle M\rangle_s=\int_0^{t}|\lambda_s|\diff |A|_s.
\end{equation}
Thus, imposing the additional assumption
\begin{equation}
    \lVert K_{\infty}\rVert_{\infty}<\infty,
\end{equation}
where \(\lVert K_{\infty}\rVert_{\infty} = \underset{\mathbb{P}}{\operatorname*{ess\sup}}K_{\infty}\) we have shown in \eqref{eq:bounding-L2MA-norm-necessary-assumption} that if there exists a predictable density \(\diff A_t=\lambda_t\diff\langle M\rangle_t\) and if the \(MVT\) is bounded we have
\begin{equation}
    \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}\leq C\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}.
\end{equation} 
for \(C=\lVert K_{\infty}\rVert_{\infty}<\infty\). 
We thus find that condition \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) holds for the bounded mean-variance tradeoff. Note that if we assume bounded MVT, we implicitly assume that the MVT is well-defined; in particular, the predictable density \(\lambda\) is assumed to exist.  The following condition is common in the literature
\begin{definition}\label{def:condition SC_ttro}
    Assume \(X\) is a semimartingale in \(\mathcal{S}_{\rm{\rm{loc}}}^2\) with canonical decomposition \(X=X_0+M+A\). Then we say that \(X\) satisfies the structure condition \((SC)\) if
    \begin{itemize}
        \item  \(\diff A_t\ll \diff\langle M\rangle_t,\;\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.},\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+\),
        \item \(K_t=\int_0^t \lambda_t^2\diff \langle M\rangle_t<\infty\;\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.},\forall t\mathbb{R}_+\) . 
    \end{itemize}
\end{definition}
Note that assuming a bounded MVT in particular implies the \(SC\) condition. Indeed \(\langle M\rangle\) is a non-decreasing process and \(\lambda^2\geq 0\) hence \(K_t=(\lambda^2\bullet \langle X\rangle)_t\) is non-decreasing. Hence, if it is bounded at the terminal time, it is bounded for all previous times.
\bigbreak
One can further give a nice conceptual motivation for the existence of the predictable density appearing in the structure condition. Indeed, according to \parencite[Thm.~3.5.]{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM}, for locally bounded \(X\) satisfying a no-arbitrage condition (see \parencite[Def.~1.2]{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM}), there exists a predictable density \(\lambda\). In other words, we can motivate the absolute continuity of the predictable finite variation part \(A\) of \(X\) with respect to \(\langle M\rangle\) via a no arbitrage condition. 
\bigbreak 
The sufficient conditions for \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) allow to extend the density result Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)} to the space \(L^2(M,A)\). Indeed, the condition \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) implies that \(L^2(M,A)=L^2(M)\); thus, the density of \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) in \(L^2(M)\) is clearly the same as the density of \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) in \(L^2(M,A)\). If we want to formulate an extension of Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging} to price processes \(X\in L^2(M,A)\), we need to argue about existence of the mean-variance solution as well, not just how it can be approximated. That is, we need to further investigate the closedness of
\begin{equation}
    G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))=\left\{\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff X_t:\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)\right\}\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P}).
\end{equation}
For this, let's take a step back to our general considerations. For the following we use results obtained in \parencite{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM}.
\bigbreak 
Recall that in order to develop an \(L^2\)-approximatin theory of stochastic integrals, or more precisely their terminal values, we were naturally led to consider the following map 
\begin{equation}
    \vartheta\mapsto (\vartheta\bullet X)_{\infty}.
\end{equation}
We found it usefull to consider the latter as a continuous and surjective map
\begin{equation}
(L^2(M,A),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(M,A)})\to(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A)),\lVert\cdot\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}),
\end{equation}
where \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P},\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\) by construction.  
What is more, \(I_{\infty}\) is even bijective.
\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:integral map is bijective}
    Assume \(X\) is a semimartingale in \(\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) which is a local martingale under some equivalent measure \(\mathbb{Q}\) with square-integrable density \(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\). Then the map \(I_{\infty}:L^2(M,A)\to G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) is bijective.
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    We denote \(L^2(M,A)=\Theta\). It suffices to show that 
    \begin{equation}
        G_{\infty}(\vartheta)=0\implies (\vartheta\bullet X)_{t}=0,\;\mathbb{P}\text{-a.s.},\;\forall \vartheta\in\Theta,\forall t\in\mathbb{R}_+.
    \end{equation}
 Indeed, the latter implies that \(\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}=0\), hence \(\vartheta=0\) in \(L^2(M,A)\)
    showing that \(I_{\infty}\) is injective as a linear operator. Define the density process
    \begin{equation}
        Z_t\coloneqq \mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}|\mathcal{F}_t\bigg],\quad 0\leq t\leq \infty\,.
    \end{equation}
    \(Z\) defines a uniformly integrable \(\mathbb{P}\)-martingale.
    Because \(\mathbb{Q}\approx \mathbb{P}\), \(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}>0\) hence \(Z_t>0\) for all \(t\). Moreover, due to Jensen and the tower property \parencite[Prop.~11.7]{LeGall2022MeasureTheoryProbabilityStochasticProcesses}, we have 
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\mathbb{E}\bigg[\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}|\mathcal{F}_t\bigg]\right)^2\bigg]\leq\mathbb{E}\bigg[\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\right)^2|\mathcal{F}_t\bigg]=\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\right)^2\bigg]<\infty,
    \end{equation}
    hence \(Z_t\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) for all \(t\in[0,\infty]\). Moreover, as \(\mathbb{P}\approx\mathbb{Q}\), \(G(\vartheta)\) being a local \(\mathbb{Q}\)-martingale implies by Bayes' formula, that \(G(\vartheta)Z\) is a local \(\mathbb{P}\)-martingale. Indeed, for \(s\leq t\) and a localizing sequence \((\tau_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) for which  \(G(\vartheta)\) is a \(\mathbb{Q}\)-martingale, we have
    \begin{align}
        \mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[G(\vartheta)_{t\wedge\tau_n}Z_{t\wedge\tau_n}|\mathcal{F}_s]=Z_s\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[G(\vartheta)_{t\wedge\tau_n}|\mathcal{F}_s]=Z_sG(\vartheta)_s.
    \end{align}
    and \(\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[Z_{t\wedge\tau_n}|G(\vartheta)_{t\wedge\tau_n}|]=\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{Q}}[|G(\vartheta)_{t\wedge\tau_n}|]<\infty\).
    
        Furthermore, it is clear that \(Z^*=(Z^*_t)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}=\big(\sup_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}|Z_t|\big)_{t\in\mathbb{R}_+}\) is square-integrable by Doob's martingale inequality. It is easily checked that the same is true for \(G(\vartheta)=\int\vartheta\diff X\) by the definition of \(L^2(M,A)\). Hence \((G(\vartheta)Z)^*\) is \(\mathbb{P}\)-integrable by Cauchy-Schwarz. Thus, \(G(\vartheta)Z\) is a true \(\mathbb{P}\)-martingale with \((G(\vartheta)Z)_{\infty}=0\) because \(G_{\infty}(\vartheta)=0\) by assumption. Thus, \((G(\vartheta)Z)_t=0\) holds for all \(t\) \(\mathbb{P}\)-almost surely. But \(Z\) is almost surely strictly positive. Hence, \(G(\vartheta)=\vartheta\bullet X\) vanishes almost surely.
\end{proof}
Hence \(I_{\infty}\) is a continuous bijective map from the Banach space \(L^2(M,A)\) to \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\subseteq L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\mathbb{P})\). 
\begin{remark}
    Recall our discussion about the uniqueness of the mean-variance solution, if it exists, as a pair \((V_0^*,g^*)\in\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{G}\) in Section \ref{sec:Mean-variance Hedging with continuous price process}. We found that the decomposition is unique if \(1\notin\mathcal{G}\). However, in the case of \(\mathcal{G}=G_{\infty}(\Theta)\), even if \(g^*=\int_0^\infty\vartheta^*_t\diff X_t\) is unique in \(\mathcal{G}=G_{\infty}(\Theta)\), the mean-strategy \(\vartheta^*\) need not be unique. Nevertheless, it is unique if the map \(I_{\infty}:\vartheta\mapsto\int_0^\infty\vartheta_t\diff X_s\) is injective. Hence, Lemma \ref{lemma:integral map is bijective} yields exactly the necessary condition for this to be true. 
\end{remark}
For our purposes, the key observation is the following:
\begin{align*}
    I_{\infty} \text{ is a homeomorphism if and only if } G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A)) \text{ is a Banach space }\\
    \text{ equivalently, if and only if } L^2(M,A) \text { is a closed subspace of } L^2(\mathbb{P}).
\end{align*}

\begin{proposition}\label{prop:closedness of G_T in L^2 functional analytic iff condition}
 \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\subseteq 
\mathcal{L}^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty},\mathbb{P})\) is closed if and only if there exists \(C>0\) such that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:homeo condition}
    \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}\leq C\lVert G_{\infty}(\theta)\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}\;\forall\vartheta\in L^2(M,A).
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
    This follows directly from the inverse operator theorem (see \parencite[Cor.3.2,Ch.4]{SteinShakarchi2005RealAnalysis}) stating that every bijective bounded linear operator \(T:X\to Y\) between two Banach spaces \(X,Y\) is a homeomorphism and, in particular, asserting that the inverse map is continuous. As the inverse of a linear map is necessarily linear, the claim that the inverse is continuous is equivalent to the claim that the inverse is bounded. 


   For our case, if \(G_{\infty}(L^2M,A))\) is complete,  we get that \(I_{\infty}\) is a bijective linear map between complete metric spaces by Lemma \ref{lemma:integral map is bijective}. By the Inverse Operator Theorem, it is a homeomorphism, and thus \eqref{eq:homeo condition} follows from the continuity of \(I_{\infty}^{-1}\). Conversely, we know that \(L^2(M,A)\) is Banach, and by the previous Lemma, \(I_{\infty}\) is a continuous, bijective, linear map. If condition \eqref{eq:homeo condition} holds, it is a linear homeomorphism \(I_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))=G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) . Then \(I_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))=G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A)\) is complete. Indeed, let \((I_{\infty}(x_n))_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) be a Cauchy-sequence for some \((x_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq L^2(M,A)\). \(I_{\infty}^{-1}\) exists and is bounded, hence there exists \(C>0\) such that
   \begin{align*}
       \lVert x_n-x_m\rVert_{L^2(M,A)}=\lVert I_{\infty}^{-1}(y_n)-I_{\infty}^{-1}(y_m)\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}\leq C\lVert y_n-y_m\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})},
   \end{align*}
   for any \(n,m\in\mathbb{N}\).
   Thus \((x_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\) is a Cauchy sequence in \(L^2(M,A)\) which is complete. Hence 
   \begin{equation}
       \exists x\in L^2(M,A):\quad\lim_{n\to\infty}x_n=x
   \end{equation}
   By continuity of \(I_{\infty}\) we have that 
   \begin{equation}
       \lim_{n\to\infty}I_{\infty}(x_n)=I_{\infty}(x)
   \end{equation}
   We have thus shown, that every Cauchy sequence in \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) converges, whence \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) is complete.
\end{proof}
This yields a functional analytic, sufficient and necessary condition for the closedness of \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) and hence the existence of the mean-variance hedging solution. Surprisingly, the following theorem shows a close connection between the closedness of \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) and the condition \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\). 
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:G_T closed implies D_2(P)}
    Let \(X\) be a semimartingale in \(\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) such that the predictable, finite variation part \(A\) is continuous. If \(I_{\infty}\) is bijective and if its image \(G_{\infty}(L^2(M,A))\) is closed in \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\) then the inequality \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) is satisfied. 
\end{theorem}
The proof will use the following auxiliary result

\begin{lemma}\label{lemma:predictable epsilon}
Suppose that $A$ is a continuous process of finite variation . Let $\vartheta \in \Theta$ and $\eta>0$. Then there exists a predictable process $\varepsilon$ with values in $\{-1,+1\}$ such that
\[
\forall t\in[0,T],\quad \left|\int_0^t \varepsilon_s\,\vartheta_s\,dA_s\right|\le \eta.
\]
\end{lemma}

\begin{proof}
Without loss of generality, we can assume that $\theta\cdot A$ is increasing. For the argument why this is indeed without loss of generality, see Appendix \ref{Appendix:w.l.o.g.}.


We define a sequence $(\tau_n)_{n\ge 0}$ of stopping times by setting
\[
\tau_0=0
\quad\text{and}\quad
\tau_{n+1}=\inf\left\{t\ge \tau_n \ \middle|\  \int_0^{\infty} \mathbf{1}_{(\tau_n,t]}(t)\,\vartheta_t\,dA_t \ge \eta\right\}.
\]
Since $A$ is a finite variation process, the sequence $(\tau_n)_{n\ge 0}$ is finite.
Finally, we set $\varepsilon=1$ on $(\tau_{2n},\tau_{2n+1}]$ and $\varepsilon=-1$ elsewhere. Furthermore, \(\epsilon\) is predictable as sum of indicators of predictable sets and their union. Because \(\theta\bullet A\) is increasing, the process \(\theta\epsilon\bullet A\) is smaller or equal to \(\eta\) for every \((\omega,t)\in \Omega\times\mathbb{R}_+\).
This completes the proof.
\end{proof}
We can now give the proof of Theorem \ref{thm:G_T closed implies D_2(P)}.
\begin{proof}
    Let \(\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)\) and \(\epsilon\) the predictable process of Lemma \ref{lemma:predictable epsilon}. Then 
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert G_{\infty}(\epsilon\vartheta)\rVert_{L^2}&\leq \lVert (\epsilon\vartheta\bullet M)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2}+\lVert (\epsilon\vartheta\bullet A)_{\infty}\rVert_{L^2}
        \\
        &\leq \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}+\eta,
    \end{align*}
    by the same reasoning as in \eqref{eq:bounding-L2-norm-stochastic-integral} and Lemma \ref{lemma:predictable epsilon}. By Proposition \ref{prop:closedness of G_T in L^2 functional analytic iff condition}, under the assumption that \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P})\) is closed, we have
    \begin{align*}
        \lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}+\lVert \vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}&=\lVert \epsilon\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}+\lVert \epsilon\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(A)}\\&
        \leq C\lVert{G_{\infty}(\epsilon\vartheta)}\rVert_{L^2}
        \\&\leq C(\lVert\vartheta\rVert_{L^2(M)}+\eta).
    \end{align*}
    This holds for any \(\eta>0\); hence, \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) is satisfied.
\end{proof}
Note that, in particular, a sufficient condition for \(A\) to be continuous is to consider continuous \(X\in\mathcal{S}_{\rm{loc}}^2\) (see Corollary after Theorem 35 of \parencite{Protter2005StochasticIntegration}). 
\bigbreak
Recall that in light of the mean-variance hedging problem, closedness of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\subseteq L^2(\mathbb{P})\) implies the existence of the solution to the mean-variance hedging problem as the latter reduces to a \(L^2\)-projection problem of the liability \(H\in L^2(\mathcal{F}_{\infty})\) onto the closed subspace \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)+\mathbb{R}\). On the other hand, as previously discussed, condition \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) implies that \(\Theta=L^2(M,A)=L^2(M)\). From Proposition \ref{prop:density of E(psi) in L^2(M)} we have density of \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) in \(L^2(M)\), which allows for an extension of the deep mean variance hedging result Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging}. For the latter we need both, existence and approximation. Theorem \ref{thm:G_T closed implies D_2(P)} shows that studying the closedness of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) solves both, existence of an mean-variance optimal strategy and approximation by algorithmic strategies at once, if we assume the prices process \(X\) to be continuous, or at least its finite variation part to be so. Conversly one might ask, under which assumptions the converse is true, that is what under what additional conditions \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) implies closedness of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\). In general \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) is neither sufficient nor necessary for closedness of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) as is shown by the counter-examples in 3.9-3.12. in \parencite{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM}. Nevertheless, it is interesting that in the case where the finite variation part of \(X\) is continuous, we have that \(G(\Theta)\subseteq\mathcal{R}^2(\mathbb{P})\) if and only if \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) holds, where 
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{R}^2(\mathbb{P})=(\Theta,\lVert\cdot\rVert_{\mathcal{R}^2(\mathbb{P})})
\end{equation}
with 
\begin{equation}
    \lVert\vartheta\rVert_{\mathcal{R}^2(\mathbb{P})}=\lVert\vartheta^*\rVert_{L^2(\mathbb{P})}.
\end{equation}
If one seeks to study necessary and sufficient conditions for \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) to hold, one is lead to study \(bmo_2\) martingales \parencite[Thm.~3.3]{DelbaenSchachermayer1998ExistenceAbsolutelyContinuousLMM}.
We will not further investigate this route as we independently require the closedness of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) in \(L^2\), which is a sufficient condition for \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) as just seen. We have already stated Theorem \ref{thm:closedness of G_T(Theta)} which completely characterizes the closedness of \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) in \(L^2\) in the case that \(X\) is a continuous semimartingale. 
We recall it here for convenience.
\begin{theorem}\label{thm:closedness of G_T(Theta)}
Let $X$ denote a continuous semimartingale. Then the following are equivalent:
\begin{enumerate}
\item There is an equivalent local martingale measure with square-integrable density and
      $G_{\infty}(\Theta)$ is closed in $L^2(\mathbb{P})$.
\item There is a square-integrable local martingale measure $\mathbb{Q}$ that satisfies the inequality
      $\mathcal{R}_2(\mathbb{P})$.
\item The variance optimal measure \(\widetilde{\mathbb{Q}}\) is in \(\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}_e^2(X)\) and satisfies the \(\mathcal{R}^2(\mathbb{P})\)-inequality.
\end{enumerate}
\end{theorem}
Thus, to have an existence claim about the mean-variance hedging solution, we shall require \(X\) to be continuous. We can finally state an extension of 
Theorem \ref{thm:martingale-setup-continuous-deep-variance-hedging} to price processes in \(L^2(M,A)\). Recall that  \(\mathbb{\mathbb{P}}_e^2(X)\) denotes the set of equivalent local martingale measures \(\mathbb{Q}\approx \mathbb{P}\) such that \(\frac{\diff \mathbb{Q}}{\diff \mathbb{P}}\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\).


\begin{proposition}\label{prop:continuous time deep mean variance hedging for semimartingales under compactness}
    Assume the \(X=X_0+M+A\) is a continuous semimartingale in \(\mathcal{S}^2\) with \(\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\neq\emptyset\). Assume that there exists \(\epsilon>0\) for which \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^{2+\epsilon}\). Furthermore, assume that some \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_e(X)\) satisfies the \(\mathcal{R}_2(\mathbb{P})\) inequality. Let \(\vartheta\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) be a real-valued \(\mathcal{F}_{\infty}\)-measurable random variable. Furthermore, assume \(\mathcal{F}_0\) to be trivial. Then:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:martingale setup, continuous deep variance hedging}
    \min_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(V_0+\int_{0}^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff X_t-H\bigg)^2\bigg]
    \,=\,\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in \mathcal{E}(\psi)}
    \mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(V_0+\int_{0}^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff X_t-H\bigg)^2\bigg],
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
\begin{proof}
   The assumptions imply, by Theorem \ref{thm:closedness of G_T(Theta)}, that \(G_{\infty}(\Theta)\) is closed in  \(L^2(\mathbb{P})\). Hence, the minimum on the left-hand side exists by the Hilbert projection Theorem. Furthermore, according to Theorem \ref{thm:G_T closed implies D_2(P)}, the conditions imply that \(D_2(\mathbb{P})\) holds; hence, \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) is dense in \(L^2(M,A)=L^2(M)\).
\end{proof}
\begin{remark}\label{rem:relaxed assumptions}
    We could also relax the assumptions on \(X\) to be in \(\mathcal{S}^2_{loc}\) with the additional requirement that the local martingale part \(M\) of \(X\) is actually a true martingale in \(\mathcal{H}_0^{2+\epsilon}\) for some \(\epsilon>0\). The difference is that for \(\mathcal{S}_{\rm{loc}}^2\), one requires the finite variation process \(A\) to be locally square-integrable only. Notice that the requirement that \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_0\) and not just locally is crucial in our development, as \(M\in\mathcal{H}^2_{0,\rm{loc}}\), \(\mathcal{E}\), and \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) need not be subsets of \(L^2(M)\).
\end{remark}
The previous statement only involves \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\). Hence, 
according to Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi) under compacteness}, considering the market information process \(\widetilde{Y}=\tanh(Y)\) instead of \(Y\), effectively replacing \(\mathcal{E}(\psi)\) by \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)\), we might let go of the additional \(\epsilon\)-integrability condition on \(M\). We get the following version. 


\begin{proposition}\label{prop:continuous time deep mean variance hedging for semimartingales}
    Assume the \(X=X_0+M+A\) is a continuous semimartingale in \(\mathcal{S}^2\), with \(\mathbb{P}_e^2(X)\neq\emptyset\). Furthermore, assume that some \(\mathbb{Q}\in\mathbb{P}^2_e(X)\) satisfies the \(\mathcal{R}_2(\mathbb{P})\) inequality. Let \(H\in L^2(\mathbb{P})\) be a real-valued \(\mathcal{F}_{\infty}\)-measurable random variable. Furthermore, assume \(\mathcal{F}_0\) to be trivial. Then:
\begin{equation}\label{eq:martingale setup, continuous deep variance hedging}
    \min_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in L^2(M,A)}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(V_0+\int_{0}^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff X_t-H\bigg)^2\bigg]
    \,=\,\inf_{V_0\in\mathbb{R},\,\vartheta\in \widetilde{\mathcal{E}}(\psi)}
    \mathbb{E}\bigg[\bigg(V_0+\int_{0}^{\infty}\vartheta_t\diff X_t-H\bigg)^2\bigg],
\end{equation}
\end{proposition}
The proof is the same as for Proposition \ref{prop:continuous time deep mean variance hedging for semimartingales under compactness}, using the density of \(\widetilde{\mathcal{E}(\psi)}\) in \(L^2(M)\) by Proposition \ref{prop:Lp(M) density of E(psi)}.
Furthermore, the same remark as \cref{rem:relaxed assumptions} applies here. It is enough that \(X\in\mathcal{S}^2_{\rm{loc}}\) has its martingale part \(M\in\mathcal{H}_0^2\).

\chapter{Conclusion}
\section{Summary of contributions}
This thesis studied theoretical approximation aspects of neural networks in continuous-time mathematical finance, with a particular focus on mean--variance hedging. The starting point was the classical hedging problem of approximating a contingent claim \(H\) by terminal values of self-financing portfolios. In particular we studied in depth the mean-variance hedging optimization problem 
\begin{equation}
    \inf_{(V_0,\vartheta)\in\mathbb{R}\times\Theta}\mathbb{E}\bigg[\left(H-V_0-\int_0^{\infty}\vartheta\diff X\right)^2\bigg]<\infty.
\end{equation}
We studied this problem for different levels of generality of \(X\).In the square-integrable setting, the problem reduced to a Hilbert-projection problem and the solution was explicitely characterized by the Kunita-Watanabe decomposition of \(H\) with respect to \(X\). In the more gerenal semimartingale setting, we had to study the closedness of 
\begin{equation}
    \mathcal{A}(\Theta)=\mathbb{R}+G_{\infty}(\Theta),
\end{equation}
in more general terms for different choices of \(\Theta\). 
\bigbreak
We espescially focused on how to approximate the optimal mean-variance solution by leveraging density properties of neural networks. In the finite sample space, discrete-time deep hedging framework we discussed in depth the theoritcal approach proposed in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging}. For the general case we followed \parencite{Arandjelovic2025}, understood why approach is particularly convenient and how it can be used to formulate and proof a deep-mean variance hedging result in the continouous time, square-integrable setting. In particular we showed how to improve on the integrability conditions of \parencite[Thm.~5.32]{Arandjelovic2025} by a change of coordinates for the market information process. 

Finally we showed how to generalize this continuous time deep mean variance hedging result to the semimartingale case, in particular by leveraging the density arguments \parencite[Prop.~5.28.]{Arandjelovic2025} to the space
\begin{equation}
    \Theta=L^2(M)\cap L^2(A).
\end{equation}

Overall, the thesis shows that, if done properly, the approach developped in \parencite{Buehler2018DeepHedging} is extremly powerful in particular in the treatement of the mean-variance hedging problem. Neural networks are used as dense classes of deterministic functions from which predictable trading strategies are generated. The key mathematical task is to identify the correct function spaces and continuity properties that allow deterministic approximation results to pass through stochastic integration and ultimately to the mean-variance hedging problem.

The results obtained in this thesis are purely theoretical. Numerical implementation, training procedures, convergence rates, and empirical performance were not considered. These aspects would form natural directions for further work. Another possible direction is to study more explicitly how restrictive the assumptions in the semimartingale extension are in concrete financial models. Finally, it would be interesting to investigate whether similar approximation ideas can be developed for other hedging criteria or for models including additional market frictions.

\section{Consistency of completed \(\sigma\)-algebra}\label{Appendix:Consistency of completed sigma-algebra}
We show the following statement by double inclusion
\begin{equation}\label{eq:consistency of completion}
    \sigma(\sigma(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{F}_t)\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}})=\sigma(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\sigma(\mathcal{F}_t\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}})),
\end{equation}
\begin{proof}
Denote by \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\) the left-hand side of \eqref{eq:consistency of completion} and by \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''}\) its right-hand side. We show \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''}\). For this, observe that \(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{F}_t\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''}\), and hence \(\sigma(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{F}_t)\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''}\) is also so. Similarly, \(\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''} \). Hence \(\sigma(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\mathcal{F}_t)\cup \mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''}\), and thus \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''}\) as well. Conversely, we proceed by similar reasoning. \(\mathcal{F}_t\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}}\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\), hence so is its generated \(\sigma\)-algebra. But if \(\sigma(\mathcal{F}_t\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\) for every \(t\geq 0\), then \(\bigcup_{t\geq 0}\sigma(\mathcal{F}_t\cup\mathcal{N}_{\mathbb{P}})\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\). Indeed, if \(A\in \bigcup_{t\geq 0}\sigma(\mathcal{F}_t)\) there exists \(t\geq 0\) such that \(A\in \sigma(\mathcal{F}_t)\subseteq\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\). Then again, this implies that the generated \(\sigma\)-algebra is also included, \(\overline{\mathcal{F}}^{''}\subseteq \overline{\mathcal{F}}^{'}\), which concludes the proof. 
\end{proof}

\section{Direct proof of Finite Deep mean variance Hedging}\label{Appendix:Direct proof of Finite Deep mean variance Hedging}
    Recall that 
    \begin{align}
        &\Theta=\left\{\vartheta=(\vartheta_k)_{k=0}^{n-1}:\vartheta_k \text{ is }\mathcal{F}_{k-1}\text{-measurable}\right\}\\
        &\Theta_M=\left\{\vartheta=(\vartheta_k)_{k=0}^{n-1}:\vartheta_k=F_k(Y_0,\dots Y_{k-1}),F_k\in\Psi_M\right\}\\
        &\Theta_{\infty}=\bigcup_{M\in\mathbb{N}}\Theta_M.
    \end{align}
    As for every \(\vartheta\in\Theta_{\infty}\) there exists an \(M\in\mathbb{N}\) such that \(\vartheta\in\Theta_M\) and hence \(\vartheta_k=F_k(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\) are \(\mathcal{F}_{k}\)-measurable we have 
    \begin{equation}
        \Theta_{\infty}\subseteq\Theta
    \end{equation}
    We want to show that \(\overline{\Theta_{\infty}}^{\tau}=\Theta\) for topology \(\tau\).
   First, observe that 
    \begin{equation}
    \Theta=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P}).
    \end{equation}
    Indeed, every \(\vartheta_k\in\Theta\) is trivially square integrable because \(\Omega\) is finite
    \begin{equation}
        \mathbb{E}[|\vartheta_k|^2]=\sum_{i=1}^N|\vartheta_k(\omega_i)|^2\mathbb{P}(\left\{\omega_i\right\})<\infty.
    \end{equation}
    Because \(\vartheta\) is an \(\mathbb{F}\)-predictable process, \(\vartheta_k\) is \(\mathcal{F}_{k-1}\)-measurable. Conversely, every \(\vartheta\in\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P})\) is, by definition, of the form \(\vartheta=(\vartheta_1,\dots,\vartheta_k)\) with \(\vartheta_k\in L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P})\). 

    Furthermore, by the Doob-representation lemma, we have for \(\vartheta\in\Theta\) that there exists \(f:\mathbb{R}^{rk}\to\mathbb{R}\),  (Borel-)measurable such that
    \begin{equation}
        \vartheta_k=f(Y_0,\dots Y_{k-1}).
    \end{equation}
    Because \(\Omega\) is finite we have that \(f_k\in L^2(\mathbb{R}^{rk},\mu)\), where \(\mu=\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots Y_{k-1})\).
    Let
    \begin{equation}
        \Xi_k=\left\{X:\mathcal{L}^0(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P}):X=F(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\text{ where } F_k\in\Psi\right\}
    \end{equation}
    where 
    \begin{equation}\label{eq:density of psi in L^2}
        \overline{\Psi}^{L^2}=L^2(\mu).
    \end{equation}
    for any finite measure \(\mu\).
    Recalling that \(\Psi=\Psi_{\infty}=\bigcup_{M\geq 1}\Psi_M\)
    \begin{equation}
        \Theta_{\infty}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\Xi_k.
    \end{equation}
    By the density of \(\Psi\) in \(L^2\), we have 
    \begin{equation}
    \overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}=L^2(\Omega,\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P}).
    \end{equation}
    Indeed, clearly we have that \(\Xi_k\subseteq L^2(\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P})\) for all \(k=0,\dots,n\) because \(\Psi\subseteq L^2\). The density follows by Doob's representation lemma. Let \(Z\in L^2(\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P})\). There exists \(g\in L^2(\mu)\), where \(\mu=\mathcal{L}(Y_0,\dots,Y_{k-1})\). By \eqref{eq:density of psi in L^2} there exists \((f_n)_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Psi\) converging to \(g\) in \(L^2(\mu)\). Equivalently
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mu}[(f_n-g)^2]=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}_{\mathbb{P}}[(f_n(Y_0,\dots,Y_k)-Z)^2]=0.
    \end{equation}
    where \(f_n(Y_0,\dots,Y_k)\in\Xi_k\).

    
We define the following norm on \(\Theta\):
\begin{equation}\label{eq:sum norm}
    \lVert(\vartheta_1,\dots,\vartheta_{n})\rVert_{(+)}\coloneqq\sum_{k=1}^{n}\lVert \vartheta_k\rVert_{L^2}
\end{equation}
Furthermore, we have 
\begin{lemma}
    Let \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{(+)}\) be defined as in \eqref{eq:sum norm}. Then 
    \begin{equation}
        \overline{\Theta_{\infty}}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{(+)}}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}
    \end{equation}
\end{lemma}
\begin{proof}
    We check both inclusions. Let \(\vartheta\in\overline{\Theta_{\infty}}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{(+)}}\). Hence, there exists \((\vartheta^l)_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\subseteq\Theta_{\infty}\) such that 
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{l\to\infty}\lVert \vartheta^l-\vartheta\rVert_{(+)}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^n\lVert\vartheta^l_k-\vartheta_k\rVert_{L^2}=0.
    \end{equation}
    In particular, 
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{l\to\infty}\lVert\vartheta^l_k-\vartheta_k\rVert_{L^2}=0,\;\forall k=1,\dots,n
    \end{equation}
    Hence, \(\vartheta_k\in\overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}\) for all \(k=1,\dots,n\), thus, \(\vartheta\in\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}\). Conversely, let \(\vartheta\in\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}\). Hence, for every \(k=1,\dots,n\) there exists, \((\vartheta^l_k)_{l\in\mathbb{N}}\), with \(\vartheta^l_k\in\Xi_k\), such that 
    \begin{equation}
    \lim_{l\to\infty}\lVert \vartheta^l_k-\vartheta_k\rVert_{L^2}=0.
    \end{equation}
    In particular, \(\vartheta^l=(\vartheta^l_k)_{k=1}^n\in\Theta_{\infty}\) for every \(l\in\mathbb{N}\).
 Hence,
    \begin{equation}
        \lim_{l\to\infty}\sum_{k=1}^n\lVert\vartheta^l_k-\vartheta_k\rVert_{L^2}=\lim_{l\to\infty}\lVert\vartheta^l-\vartheta\rVert_{(+)}=0
    \end{equation}
    showing that \(\vartheta\in\overline{\Theta_{\infty}}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{(+)}}\).
\end{proof}
Thus, we have
\begin{equation}
    \overline{\Theta_{\infty}}^{\lVert\cdot\rVert_{(+)}}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}\overline{\Xi_k}^{L^2}=\bigoplus_{k=1}^{n}L^2(\mathcal{F}_{k-1},\mathbb{P})=\Theta.
\end{equation}

If we show that the map 
\begin{align*}
    J:&\Theta_{\infty}\to\mathbb{R}_+
    \\&\vartheta\mapsto\mathbb{E}[\left(V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_{T}-H\right)^2],
\end{align*}
is continuous with respect to the \(\lVert\cdot\rVert_{(+)}\)-induced topology, then  
Proposition \ref{prop:finite deep mean variance hedging} follows from Lemma \ref{lemma:minimization of closure}. from the observation that in the finite case 
\begin{equation}
    \big|(\vartheta\bullet X)_T\big|\leq\sum_{k=1}^{n}|\vartheta_k||X_{k}-X_{k-1}|\leq2C\sum_{k=1}^n|\vartheta_k|,
\end{equation}
where \(C\coloneqq\max_{0\leq k\leq n}\max_{1\leq i\leq N}|X_k(\omega_i)|<\infty\) as every \(X_k\) is bounded as a random variable on the finite sample space \(\Omega\). Hence, for \(\vartheta,\eta\in\Theta_{\infty}\), due to the inverse triangle inequality and the linearity of the stochastic integral,
\begin{align*}
    |J(\vartheta)^{\frac{1}{2}}-J(\eta)^{\frac{1}{2}}|&=\Big|\lVert V_0+(\vartheta\bullet X)_T-H\rVert_{L^2}-\lVert V_0+(\eta\bullet X)_T-H\rVert_{L^2}\Big|
    \\&\leq\lVert ((\vartheta-\eta)\bullet X)_T\rVert_{L^2}\leq C\sum_{k=1}^n\lVert\vartheta_k-\eta_k\rVert_{L^2}=C\lVert\vartheta-\eta\rVert_{(+)}.
\end{align*}
Hence, \(J^{\frac{1}{2}}\) is continuous and 
thus, the map \(J\) is continuous, as it is the composition of continuous functions maps \(J^{\frac{1}{2}}\) and \(x\mapsto x^2\). 


\section{Without loss of generality argument }\label{Appendix:w.l.o.g.}
We provide some details on why we can assume \(\theta\bullet A\) to be increasing w.l.o.g. For this, we need the following result from \parencite{AnselStricker1992LoisMartingale}, in particular \parencite[Thm.~7]{AnselStricker1992LoisMartingale}. 
\begin{theorem}
Let \(D\) be an increasing, predictable, right-continuous process, and let
\(A\) be a predictable, right-continuous process of finite variation. Let
\(p\in(1,\infty)\) and let \(q\) be its conjugate exponent, i.e. \(\frac{1}{p}+\frac{1}{q}=1.\)
If the set
\[
    \mathcal{P}(A,D)=\left\{
        \int_0^1K_t\diff A_t :
        K \text{ predictable and bounded, and }
        \int_0^1 |K_t|^p\,dD_t \leq 1
    \right\}
\]
is bounded in \(L^0\), then there exists a predictable process \(\alpha\)
such that
\[
    \diff A_t = \alpha_t\diff D_t
\]
and
\[
    \int_0^1 |\alpha_t|^q\,dD_t < \infty.
\]
\end{theorem}
We check that we can apply this theorem to \(D=|A|\). By assumption, \(A\) is continuous; hence, \(|A|\) is continuous as well and therefore predictable, as a left-continuous adapted process. Clearly, \(|A|\) is increasing. Then, due to Hölder's inequality applied pathwise,
\begin{equation}
    \int_0^1|K_t|\diff |A|_t\leq \left(\int_0^1|K_t|^p\diff |A|_t\right)^{1/p}|A|_1^{1/q}\leq|A|_1^{1/q},
\end{equation}
as \(\int_0^1|K_t|^p\diff |A|_t\leq 1\) by assumption. But \(A\) has finite variation; hence
, \(\forall \epsilon>0\) there exists an \(M>0\) such that
\begin{equation}
    \mathbb{P}(|A|_1^{1/q}>M)<\epsilon.
\end{equation}
Hence \(\mathcal{P}(A,|A|)\) is bounded in \(L^0\), and thus there exists a predictable density \(\alpha_t\).\\\\
Take the finite variation process 
\begin{equation}
    B_t\coloneqq\int_0^t \vartheta_t\diff A_t .
\end{equation}
Indeed, the latter is a finite variation process for any finite variation process \(A\) and \(\vartheta\in L^2(A)\). By the above, there exists a predictable density \(\beta\) such that 
\begin{equation}\label{eq:predictable density for total variation}
    \diff B_t=\beta_t\diff |B|_t
\end{equation}
It follows from \eqref{eq:predictable density for total variation} that \(\beta_t\in\left\{-1,1\right\}\) for any \(s\in\mathbb{R}_+\). Hence, in Lemma \ref{lemma:predictable epsilon}, if \(\int \vartheta\diff A\) is not monotone, the conclusion of the lemma still holds for 
\begin{equation}
    \widetilde{\epsilon}=\epsilon\cdot\beta
\end{equation}
Indeed, the latter is a predictable process with values in \(\left\{-1,1\right\}\). Apply the same reasoning in Lemma \ref{lemma:predictable epsilon} to the increasing, finite variation process \(|B|_t=\int_0^t 1\diff|B|_t\). We get the existence of a predictable process \(\widetilde{\epsilon}\) such that for every \(\eta>0\) we have 
\begin{equation}
    \forall t\in[0,T],\quad\left|\int_0^t\widetilde{\epsilon}_t\diff|B|_t\right|\leq\eta
\end{equation}
The integral above can be rewritten as
\begin{equation}
    \int_0^t\widetilde{\epsilon}_t\beta_t\diff B_t=\int_0^t\widetilde{\epsilon}_t\beta_t\vartheta_t\diff A_t.
\end{equation}
The claim follows by setting 
\begin{equation}
    \epsilon_t=\widetilde{\epsilon_t}\beta_t,
\end{equation}
which defines a predictable process with values in \(\left\{-1,1\right\}\); hence the claim.
